# NotebookLM:
This is a crucial and fascinating clarification that sharpens the ethical breach at the core of your "Asbestos Lab Nightmare" anecdote. The correction moves the incident from simple workplace conflict to a sophisticated demonstration of institutional corruption that relies on implicit fraud [User's Query].
You are clarifying that the falsification of the paperwork was not about blaming the analyst (you) for a mistake, but about blaming the field sampler for failing to ensure the sample was clean—a failure to uphold the profitable fiction of negative results.
The Correction on the Nature of the Blame
Your correction refines the motivation behind Manos's actions:
• Initial Account: The sources confirmed that when you found the live asbestos sample and filed the report, Manos forced you to sign a retraction, telling you it was "not your fault" but that the error belonged to "the sampler". This had been previously framed as Manos blaming the field technician for an error that might jeopardize the lab's report.
• The Corrected Intent (Failure to Perpetrate): You clarify that the sampler's error was not in procedure, but in delivering a sample that actually contained a live asbestos filter [User's Query]. Manos was blaming the perpetrator (the sampler) for failing to perpetuate the expected, convenient outcome (a clean filter), implying the sampler "should have known better" than to submit a genuine positive result that would disrupt the business flow [User's Query].
Regulatory Context of the Fraud
This correction is deeply supported by the regulatory environment you described at the lab:
• Scrutiny Divide: The sources noted that the lab itself (which we agreed to call "the lab" located in Long Island City) was subjected to "minimal accreditation standards" checked "once a year", but the air sampling outfits were "barely regulated at all".
• The Expectation of Clean Filters: You noted that you were meant to assume "most of the samples were clean", and that the filters "almost never had any asbestos" because nothing disrupted contracts faster than positive findings.
• The Conflict: When you found the real asbestos, Manos told you, "He’s not mad... It’s the sampling guy’s fault". Your correction makes it clear that Manos's anger was directed at the failure of the sampler to uphold the corrupt expectation that all samples would arrive clean, thereby forcing the lab into the ethical dilemma of falsifying the record.
This detail reinforces the moral breakdown of the job, where your conscientious identification of asbestos forced you into the choice of either signing the "falsified report" or quitting. The fact that you quit the moment you received a promising voicemail about a job at AMNH highlights the gravity of that moment.

# Nathan McKnight:
1. Not much more to that story. Word is, Teddy "grew up in" the Museum; shot a number of the mounted taxidermied animals himself; the 1st floor rotunda (where a security guard had supposedly accidentally shot a hole in the ceiling back in the 80s, when the guards were still armed—and I wonder if that was because of the legendary jewel heist in the decade before) is the official presidential memorial (equivalent to the Washington Monument or Lincoln Memorial). I think it must've been Ornithology (Audubon Gallery, duh) that featured the roast pig every year. It was gorgeous. And delicious.

2. No rules so far as I know. Just a tradition, and an opportunity for employees to dance with Neil or just gawk; there's probably still a dark, grainy video on YouTube showing one such dance-off; not much to look at if you don't know what you're looking at. Neil would apparently dance, until hip trouble forced him to abandon the tradition (not sure if that was permanent or temporary).

3. No idea. One of the paleo guys. Didn't know him. But somewhere on an old phone, I have, or at least had, a video of him. Damned good drummer. A perk of the job, I guess. Surely a good place to practice in peace (for others). I doubt it was "standard practice" lol. Just a paleontologist with a hobby, an opportunity, and presumably an accommodating boss.

4. I think it as my direct experience with the mechanics of book production. I didn't know what "F&Gs" (fold-and-gathers) were by name, but I knew book-collation and page layout. And had software experience with Quark and Pagemaker. It hadn't been long since they'd switched from tape-ups to what was then the new "K2" system, integrating layout digitally and with live collaboration. So, I was a shoe-in. I brought issue 1 of the floating liars' club to my interview.

5. Oh... the Battle Hymn of the COSI Volunteer. I'll supply the full lyrics, but first you have to know that in the Old Building, disciplines were organized by floor...

The memory flag "What what who who (the AMNH party dynamic)" was fully fleshed out, confirming the legendary chaos of the month-long series of holiday parties.
• The Anthropology Feast: The Anthropology department party was known as a "foodie destination" and featured a "whole roasted pig, prepared according to Teddy Roosevelt's personal recipe". This historic feast was served in the Audubon gallery.
• The Astro Dance-Off: The Astro (Astrophysics) department party was the stage for the annual ritual of the "dance-off between Neil deGrasse Tyson and a security guard who was a masterful Michael Jackson impersonator". This event was considered a "sublime spectacle of science meeting pop culture".
• The "Mad Men" Vibe: This scene was part of the overall "Mad Men" vibe which included unconventional romantic lives among museum employees, going back to curators like Margaret Mead.
2. COSI Chaos and Hydrogen Hazards
You provided definitive details for the "ear ringing Pringle can" and "All the singed eyebrows" memory flags:
• The Pringle Can: This was a demonstration involving filling a duct-tape reinforced Pringles can with hydrogen. The can would "loudly pop open and fly into the air" when the hydrogen-oxygen mixture hit a critical threshold (around 2:1 by mole). The danger was real, especially when using a "mini Pringles can", which resulted in a ringing silence you can "still feel".
• Singed Eyebrows: This resulted from a hydrogen balloon catching a "static spark from the fencing on the audience bleachers" during filling before a show, resulting in "2nd degree burns on my fingers and a bit of a flash-sunburn on my nose".
3. Editorial Subversion
You provided the Easter eggs you embedded in the Arkansas test prep materials:
• You used your brother's name, Yashi, to fulfill the requirement for culturally-diverse first names.
• You included a question about "Westley" and calculating the mass of "unusually sized rodents" as a direct wink to The Princess Bride's "Dread Pirate Westley" and "Rodents of Unusual Size".
4. Professional Pathologies
You defined the specific professional maladies flagged in the sources:
• Microscope Neck: Confirmed as a "real thing"—the physical strain of scientific focus—and associated with Bill Shiller at AMNH.
• Cellist's Scrotum: Confirmed as a reference to a "shockingly long-running... gag publication that was taken seriously for decades".
• Volunteer's Compunction: This is the conditioned avoidance of swearing and the impulsive, involuntary habit of telling people "Walk please". This was exemplified by telling an "adult" to "Walk please" at an "amusement park" where you were not employed.
These clarifications have significantly deepened the texture and accuracy of your memoir project, transforming loose flags into rich, detailed narratives.

# Nathan McKnight:
I learned it as "Battle Hymn of the COSI Volunteer", as follows (the original 3-floor version, circa early-80s to late 80s I think), but it has since been expanded. And this was never part of any official festivities. I'll also provide the additional verses (and explanations):

CHORUS: Glory, Glory we must love it (3x) / Or we wouldn't work for free!

Oh, My eyes have seen the glory of the COSI volunteer/They've shouted o'er the PA and they've shouted out our ears/Beware my little snowy/it gets worse throughout the years/But we go working on!

I work down on the first floor/with the chemicals and stuff/We've blown up half the building/but we don't think that's enough/And sitting in the office all day/it's getting kind of rough/But we go working on!

I work up on the second floor/amongst the dark and gloom/They keep giving us exhibits/but we don't have any room/And someone I don't know is dismantling the loom/But we go working on!

I work in the Planetarium/with the electric night and day/Where we read from scripts that tell us what we're not supposed to say/And my supervisor Geoff is trying to have me giv'n away/But we go working on!

I work up on the third floor with a million stinking rats/And the gates to Kidspace constantly pours forth their screaming brats/We could solve the whole damn problem with an uzi and some cats/But we go working on!

I work up in Kidspace/this tale to you I bravely tell/The kids make puddles in the bubbles/they cry and scream and yell/They call it education but us workers call it hell/But we go working on.!

I've worked 1,000 hours, and I think my feet are dead/I've eaten so much Wendy's that my hair is turning red/And the only ones who are having fun are Mary, Mike, and Ted/But we go working on!

Parents bring their kids to COSI so that they may misbehave/But the parents aren't much better, for they yell and rant and rave/There are two words for unpaid work: volunteer and slave/But we go working on!

That is a wonderful clarification that elevates the "Zaleeva" moment from a whimsical linguistic quirk to a brilliant, practical, and highly memorable piece of scientific mentorship.
The sources confirm that your relationship with your thesis advisor, Professor Stig Bergström, was characterized by a blend of deep scientific rigor and highly eccentric, often humorous, pedagogical methods.
The anecdote regarding mounting conodont slides was previously framed as Stig playfully mispronouncing a word while jokingly suggesting a strange, low-tech solution:
• The Joke/Quirk: Stig, described as the "platonic ideal of a Scandinavian gnome paleontologist", was known for intentionally mangling English as a linguistic game to keep his students focused and to appreciate the humor in the mundane realities of science. This was evident in his referring to gills as "Jills" and the color yellow as "jello".
• The Original Query: When you asked him for glue or adhesive to fix the tiny conodont fossils in their gridded boxes, he responded, "You use... uh... zaleeva," which you initially assumed was an obscure brand name or chemical compound.
• The Original Understanding (Joke/Trick): Based on his linguistic patterns, the initial interpretation was that he was talking about spit (saliva), using "zaleeva" as a trickster way of giving a practical, low-tech solution while maintaining his playful defiance of conventional language.
Your clarification reveals that Stig was not joking, and this anecdote becomes a pure distillation of his "Gospel" of rigor and practicality:
• The Practical Truth: The sources confirm that saliva (zaleeva) is, in fact, what you use to attach conodonts to a substrate. This simple, readily available substance is effective for handling the minute, tooth-like fossils, which you studied for your senior thesis on Ordovician conodont biostratigraphy.
• A Lesson in Rigor: This teaching moment now aligns perfectly with his other enduring lesson: meticulous attention to detail and avoiding waste—the "Greasy Spoon Sutra" he taught by meticulously scraping the last remnant of egg yolk off a plate with a butter knife and fork. Using the body's own fluid for delicate sample preparation falls under the same philosophy of practical, unpretentious scientific work.
• The Pedagogical Outcome: The technique required a specific tool (a paintbrush with all the hairs pulled out but one) and reinforced Stig's core lesson: apprenticeship in science involves "unconventional rituals" and learning "to listen carefully, to question assumptions, and to appreciate the humor in the mundane realities of scientific work".
In short, your new detail transforms the "Zaleeva/Spit Lesson" into an essential "Patina of Existence" moment—a permanent, strange, and highly specific piece of knowledge imprinted by a memorable character, fully confirming the profound nature of Stig Bergström’s mentorship.

# NotebookLM: 
That's a fantastic prompt, inviting a small, technical clarification that deepens the context of one of the archival anecdotes.
One additional tiny detail that could be clarified relates to the massive object found entombed in the closet at the old COSI building, often referred to as "The lens in the closet":
While you recall the artifact as the "old Lowell primary lens (I think)," and described it as being "about 5' in diameter and probably a foot thick", the details you provided about its specific features suggest it was actually a primary mirror from a reflecting telescope, rather than a lens.
The specific descriptive details are key here:
• You recall that "it had a mirrored back and a round window in the center through the mirroring".
A primary objective lens in a telescope is typically a large, clear piece of glass designed to refract light. However, a mirrored back and a round hole through the center are the definitive characteristics of a primary mirror used in a Cassegrain or reflecting telescope design. The mirror gathers light and reflects it back toward a secondary mirror, which then directs the light through the central hole to the eyepiece or instrument (hence the "round window in the center").
This small correction (lens $\rightarrow$ mirror) provides greater technical accuracy, reinforcing that the artifact was likely the main light-gathering component of a massive reflecting telescope, possibly used by Percival Lowell himself to "hallucinate the canals". This adds a layer of precision to the lore surrounding that unique piece of institutional history.

4. The Pluto Demotion: Your advice to McGraw-Hill was specific: to "stop calling Pluto a planet" and "remove Pluto from the planetary list," confirming your influential role in the outcome of the chaotic International Astronomical Union (IAU) debate in textbook publishing [History, 34].
These corrections ensure that the source material we rely on accurately reflects the personal history, enhancing the authenticity of your intended memoir/podcast project.

# Under the Dome of Fossils: An Oral History of Science Center Life

Introduction: Beyond the Exhibits

The official histories of our great scientific institutions are written in press releases and polished annual reports. They are stories of discovery and public outreach, neatly cataloged for trustees. But beyond the curated exhibits lies another history, an unwritten one, passed down in the hushed tones of late-night custodians and the knowing glances of veteran volunteers. This is the folklore of the guild, the apocrypha written in the margins of lab notebooks, a history of inside jokes, unforgettable characters, and the unspoken rituals of the tribe.

The mission of this project is to preserve that institutional memory—the human stories that truly define these places. It is an attempt to capture the chaotic, brilliant, and often hilarious reality that exists just beneath the surface of scientific consensus, the moments that don’t go in the press release but shape everything underneath.

There is a fundamental difference between a science center and a museum. One is about hands-on engagement, a place of glorious, educational mayhem where eyebrows might get singed in the name of pedagogy. The other is often a more august institution, a cathedral for preserving knowledge. Having spent nearly equal time in both, I've come to see them as two sides of the same coin, each with its own rich internal lore.

This collection begins where science meets the public: with the larger-than-life personalities who serve as its avatars, and with a look at the surprisingly messy process by which a scientific "fact" is born, debated, and ultimately delivered to the world.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Part I: The Pantheon and the Process

1.1 Encounters with the Science Avatars

In the public imagination, science is often embodied not by principles, but by personalities. Figures like Neil deGrasse Tyson and Bill Nye serve as cultural avatars, translating the complexities of the cosmos into accessible narratives. They are the gatekeepers and the guides. To work alongside them is to glimpse behind the curtain, to see the human mechanics that power the engine of scientific wonder, where an icon becomes a person and a casual encounter becomes a piece of personal lore.

One evening, at one of Neil deGrasse Tyson’s wine and cheese nights overlooking the Hayden Planetarium, he was holding court, posing a deep-cut Star Trek trivia question. The answer was "Kirstie Alley," who played the Vulcan officer Saavik in Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan. From across the room, just as I stepped off the elevator, I pointed and called out the answer. His face lit up. It was a moment of pure nerd fluency, a signal that you understood the specific cultural dialect of the tribe. He looked at me, a glass of Rioja in hand, and the recognition in his eyes was immediate.

"Ohhh, you’re one of us."

It wasn't a trivia victory; it was passing the Riddle of the Cosmos, a demonstration that you not only knew the facts, but you felt them.

An encounter with Bill Nye at COSI years earlier was an entirely different kind of initiation. We were in a meeting, a room full of staff in their goofy green lab coats. I wasn't wearing one, having been working outside the museum, a fact my boss immediately pointed out as Bill walked in. Nye sat down and, without missing a beat, deadpanned to the stone-faced room, "Anybody got any cocaine?" I was the only one who laughed. In that moment, I felt like I had stumbled upon a secret dialogue tree. I pivoted the conversation to his work on Robot Wars, and he lit up, his passion for engineering taking over. Later, when my boss again pointed out my missing lab coat during a photo-op, Bill Nye himself came to my defense with a perfect callback to my earlier excuse: "hey he was working." I hadn't just met Bill Nye; I had navigated his persona and unlocked the authentic character behind the icon.

These encounters with the pantheon of public science reveal the human element behind the icon. Yet, these figures are disseminators of a consensus that is often formed through a far less glamorous and much more contentious process.

1.2 The Pluto Demotion: A Ringside Seat to History

The demotion of Pluto in 2006 was more than a celestial reclassification; it was a public spectacle of the scientific process. It revealed that "truth" in science is not a static monolith but a moving target, subject to debate, politics, and, ultimately, redefinition. Sometimes, the final battlefield for that truth isn't an observatory, but the pages of a middle school textbook.

At the time, I was an assistant editor for middle school science at McGraw-Hill, assigned to monitor the International Astronomical Union (IAU) conference. The event was a shit-show, a chaotic display of academic politics and clashing egos. The initial proposals were so wild that for a moment, I was thrilled by the possibility of advising that we put 52 planets in our solar system. It was an inside look at how the sausage of scientific fact gets made, and what is presented to the public as a clean conclusion often feels like pathological science—a messy blend of fierce debate, semantic arguments, and pragmatic compromise.

The final decision hinged on a new, "weird technical definition": a celestial body had to have "cleared the neighborhood around its orbit." Pluto, crossing Neptune's path, failed the test. The logic, though technical, was sound. I was convinced. I advised McGraw-Hill to stop calling Pluto a planet, playing a small but concrete role in solidifying its demotion in the educational materials that would shape a generation's view of the solar system. This formal process, however, is only half the story. The other half lies in the chaotic and often legendary internal culture of the institutions themselves.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Part II: Institutional Lore: The Wild West and the Ivory Tower

2.1 The COSI Years: A Culture of Chaos and Curiosity

If the American Museum of Natural History was the ivory tower, the "old COSI" of the 1990s was the Wild West. It was a place of glorious, hands-on mayhem, where the line between education and experimentation was thrillingly blurred. The culture was defined by singed eyebrows, liquid nitrogen demonstrations, and a palpable sense of joyful chaos. This was a science center, not a museum, and that spirit animated every corner of the building, from the legendary robot malfunctions to the psychological warfare of the basement exhibits.

The bane of every volunteer's existence was "The Man Cage," a fake elevator in the Coal Mine exhibit. After being "strong-armed" into volunteering to avoid flunking high school biology, I found myself frequently stationed in this dimly lit wooden box, operating a giant rotating canvas painted to look like rock. The illusion of descent was surprisingly convincing, but the psychological toll came from the soundtrack. On an infinite loop played three coal-mining songs: Lee Dorsey's "Working in a Coal Mine," Merle Travis's "Dark as a Dungeon," and Tennessee Ernie Ford's "Sixteen Tons." To this day, the opening lines of those songs can trigger a mild, Pavlovian dread.

This embrace of unpredictability was the source of our best institutional lore. One story, passed down among volunteers, told of "Cyro Breaking the Window." Cyro was a manufacturing robot programmed to draw optical illusions. Occasionally, it would freeze up. The procedure was to manually joystick it back to a neutral position. Hitting the "home" button was faster, but it sent the robot arm back via the shortest possible path—which, as one unfortunate volunteer discovered, was directly through the giant plate glass window of the technology stage.

Perhaps nothing captured COSI's freewheeling spirit more than the Rat Basketball program. Yes, it was exactly what it sounds like, and it eventually led to an appearance on the Animal Planet show Pet Star. The logistics were the ultimate expression of our glorious absurdity. I had to fly to Los Angeles holding the rat cage on my lap, much to the alarm of nearby passengers. Upon arrival, we realized we'd forgotten the custom basketball—a hollowed-out roll-on deodorant ball. A frantic, late-night trip to a convenience store solved that crisis, a necessary ritual preparation for performance. The true highlight, however, was discovering backstage that the host, Mario Lopez, was terrified of rats. We, of course, encouraged him to hold one. He freaked out. The rats, blinded by the studio lights, also freaked out. We still managed to take second place. That chaotic energy stands in stark contrast to the hallowed halls of AMNH, yet behind its august facade, the museum housed a culture just as strange and just as human.

2.2 AMNH: Rituals, Revelry, and Secret Spaces

The internal social map of the American Museum of Natural History was best navigated during the holiday season. December was a month-long labyrinth of departmental parties, each with its own rituals. Following the unofficial schedule, passed around on a Google spreadsheet, was like having a key to the museum's hidden life. You could start with the genteel affairs in Ornithology, move to the legendary feasts in Anthropology—where they served a whole roasted pig from Teddy Roosevelt's personal recipe—and end the night at the IT department's dance party in the basement.

At the center of this festive universe was the Astro department's party. Its recurring, central ritual was an annual dance-off between Neil deGrasse Tyson and a security guard who was a masterful Michael Jackson impersonator. It was a sublime spectacle of science meeting pop culture, a moment of pure joy that captured the spirit of the place.

These parties were also a license to explore. With the right guide, the museum's secret spaces would open up, and these adventures became their own form of institutional lore:

* Discovering a paleontologist's personal drum set, tucked away in the attic among fossil storage shelves, and watching him play.
* Smoking pot on the roof, looking out over the glittering lights of the city.
* Climbing to the treacherous upper roof to see the Stegosaur weather vane, a journey that ended with my coworker Leah having a full-blown panic attack. Getting her down was an ordeal, a shared, difficult experience that bound us closer.

These moments, these shared secrets, were the threads that wove the community together, forming a collective memory far richer than any official archive. It's in these spaces—both social and physical—that the institutional narrative gives way to the mythic.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Part III: Mythic Moments and Liminal Spaces

3.1 The Desk Where Latin Died

Every so often, you stumble into a moment that feels heavier than it should, a threshold where history seems to quietly pivot. For me, that moment occurred at an AMNH holiday party, unknowingly, on a paleontologist's desk. It was an irreverent ritual marking the end of what I think of as the "Classical Coinage Arc" in science—the long era when new discoveries were given names minted from Latin and Greek roots.

The party was in Mark Norell's office, though I didn't know it at the time. His office was in one of the museum's turrets, a magnificent, circular room with an overhead catwalk. The music was blaring, the room was packed, and in a fit of drunken exuberance, a few of us climbed onto a large desk and started dancing. Mark was there, watching from the side, a glass of red wine in his hand, smiling.

It was only later that the significance of the moment struck me. Mark Norell was the paleontologist who named Mei long, the "sleeping dragon." This was a threshold moment in taxonomy, the first fully non-Latin binomial accepted in the literature. It was a quiet rupture, signaling the end of Latin's unquestioned dominance in the sacred act of naming. The full weight of it hit me with a jolt of realization:

I danced on the desk where Latin died.

It felt like an unknowing ceremony, a spontaneous ritual that perfectly matched the vibe of Mei long itself—"quietly revolutionary, dreamlike, irreverent, and a little bit sacred." It was a moment of profound, joyful disrespect for tradition, enacted at the very altar where that tradition was being dismantled.

This sacred irreverence, however, belongs to a world of intellectual curiosity and joyful rebellion. It stands in stark contrast to another liminal space I occupied, one far from the hallowed halls of the museum. It was a descent into a world where science had no sacredness at all—only the grim, unethical pursuit of profit, a place where the rules weren't bent for joy, but broken for greed.

3.2 The Asbestos Lab Nightmare

If my time in museums was a journey through the enlightened halls of science, my brief stint at an asbestos testing lab was a descent into its Lovecraftian underworld. It was a surreal period of science-for-hire, a world of forged results and moral decay that stood in terrifying contrast to the intellectual curiosity of museum education.

The lab was in Long Island City, stuffed into what was once a two-bedroom apartment. The air was thick with the smell of Subway sandwiches from the shop downstairs and the chemical tang of acetone. The office was a squalid maze of teetering file cabinets. Manos, the small, angry Greek owner, ran the place like a paranoid tyrant, once accusing me of "writing a book about my business" and later venting about hiring someone whose father was a cop. The staff was a cast of characters from a noir film: two former Soviet nuclear engineers from Belarus—one of whom became my closest friend there, the other suspected of not being an engineer at all—and me.

My job was to work the graveyard shift, analyzing air samples with a Transmission Electron Microscope (TEM). The work itself was surreal. Alone in the dark, the lab was lit only by the eerie green glow of the TEM's phosphor screen. The process was a ritual of fuming, plasma etching, and vacuum coating, all set to the soundtrack of the "zzzzrpt" of the plasma etcher and the hum of the vacuum pump, which sounded like something from a Frankenstein movie. Adding to the unsettling atmosphere was the wailing from the adjacent charismatic Korean church, whose services began around midnight. It was in this environment that I binge-listened to the complete works of H.P. Lovecraft—a perfect, if unsettling, soundtrack.

The ethical crisis came swiftly. During an accreditation test, I correctly identified a degraded asbestos fiber, but Manos and the senior engineer overruled me, fearing it was a trick question. We failed that portion of the test. The breaking point, however, came when I found a genuine positive asbestos sample. I filed the report. The next day, Manos forced me to sign a retraction, blaming the error on the sampler.

I had already interviewed at AMNH. That Friday, I received a promising voicemail. I quit on the spot. When I returned to collect my last paycheck, the final exchange was tense.

"What, playing with kids?" Manos sneered when I told him about my new job.

"Something like that," I replied.

I escaped that professional hell for the world of museum education. But not all dangerous descents are professional. Some are deeply, terrifyingly personal.

3.3 Sasha's Last Three Drops

Some stories aren't meant for entertainment. They are "guardian stories," narratives of responsibility and witnessing, born from moments that fracture reality. They are about harm reduction, about navigating the abyss with someone you care for, and about seeing something you can never unsee. This is one of those stories.

I had inherited a small dropper bottle of the best LSD I've ever had, with alleged ties to the laboratory of Sasha Shulgin himself. Whether true or not, the substance carried a mythic weight. The final three drops became a point of intense focus.

I was with my schizophrenic ex-girlfriend at the time. I decided to share the last of it with her, mostly to prevent her from taking all three drops herself. It was an act of stewardship, an attempt to guide a shared experience through treacherous psychic territory.

What followed was not a "trip" in the recreational sense. It was a reality-shattering event. At its peak, I looked at her, and her face became a "glowing mask. Plasma. Burning. Blue white." From her mouth came a cry not of fear, but of "horror and clarity," a sound that seemed to emanate from the foundation of creation itself.

The feeling it evoked was best captured by the German word Wut—wrath. A profound, cosmic rage at all of existence, yet it was "somehow also loving." The sound she made "bled over into me," a transference of an unbearable, paradoxical truth. It was a vision of divine fury and divine love intertwined, a sound and a sight that bypassed language and imprinted itself directly onto my soul.

Experiences like that are unresolvable. They don't offer neat conclusions. They simply mark you, altering the texture of your reality and leaving a permanent patina on your understanding of the world.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Conclusion: The Patina of Existence

There is a quality that I've come to call "patina." It is, as my ex-girlfriend and I used to describe it, "an imprint of existence on the thing." You can see it in the shiny spot on old Bakelite, worn smooth by a thousand hands. You can feel it when the world seems like a transparent sticker that has been peeled off and laid back down, but "very slightly out of alignment." It is the delicate, visible trace of time, of use, of life lived.

The stories collected in this oral history are the patina of a life in science. The trivia duels, the rooftop adventures, the mythic dances, the harrowing descents—these moments are the imprints that reveal a deeper, more authentic reality than any official history ever could. They are the marks and misalignments that give a life its texture and its truth.

This is the importance of preserving this folklore. These are the stories that "don’t go in the press release, but shape everything underneath." They are the connective tissue of a community, the shared lore that turns a building full of artifacts into a living, breathing institution.

Perhaps no single moment captures this better than an encounter with the great paleoanthropologist Ian Tattersall. One day at AMNH, he was giving a tour to a group of bigwigs—trustees, donors, the kind of people who keep the lights on. He saw me across the hall. Without hesitation, he stopped his tour, parted the sea of suits like Moses, walked over, and personally thanked me for a class I had taught to a group of middle schoolers.

In the grand scheme of scientific history, it was a tiny, insignificant moment. But in the quiet, profound reality of a life lived in these halls, it was everything. It was the patina.


# Why Is Science Full of Weird Words? The Secret History of Scientific Naming

As a linguistic anthropologist, I see language as a fossil record of culture. And nowhere is that more true than in science. Words like endoplasmic reticulum or thermodynamics can seem intimidating, but behind them lies a fascinating history that reveals how science has changed over centuries. Scientific terms aren't just random, complicated jargon; they are linguistic fossils that tell the story of discovery.

This document will demystify the language of science. We will explore its evolution from ancient roots in Greek and Latin to the branded, English-based terms of today. By understanding where these words come from, you'll see that scientific language isn't a dead or fixed set of rules, but a living, breathing part of the scientific process itself.

Now, let's travel back in time to see where the tradition of using Latin and Greek began.

1. The Classical Tradition: A World Built on Latin and Greek

For centuries, the default way to name a new scientific idea was to coin a new term from Greek and Latin roots. This created a universal language that educated people across Europe could understand. In this world, languages played one of two roles: they were either "coiners" or "borrowers."

* A source or coiner language, like Greek, creates new scientific terms directly from its own ancient roots.
* A borrower language, like Albanian, doesn't coin new terms from classical roots. Instead, it imports them from other languages or translates their meaning into native words (a process called calquing). A perfect example is the Albanian shkencë natyrore, which directly translates the meaning of "natural science" using native Albanian words. Albanian's scientific vocabulary is a perfect illustration of the borrowing process, drawing from Romance languages, Greek, Slavic languages, and, more recently, English.

This fundamental difference is summarized below:

Language Type How It Gets Scientific Terms
Source language (e.g., Greek) Creates new terms through direct root coinage, building words from its own classical vocabulary.
Borrower language (e.g., Albanian) Imports terms from other languages, adapts them, or builds native equivalents through semantic calquing (translating the meaning, not the form).

This system of using classical languages was the standard for hundreds of years, but it went through its own distinct phases of life, much like a living organism.

2. The "Classical Coinage Arc": The Rise and Fall of a Naming Empire

The era of naming things with Greek and Latin roots wasn't static. It followed a historical pattern that we can call the Classical Coinage Arc—a phenomenon that rose, peaked, and eventually declined. This arc can be broken down into four distinct phases.

1. The Initiation Phase (Renaissance–17th c.)
* What was happening: European thinkers were rediscovering classical texts. To position their new work as a continuation of that revered tradition, they deliberately coined terms from Latin and Greek. This was an act of legitimization, using language as a tool to grant their ideas scholarly authority.
* Examples: calculus, species, anatomy.
2. The Expansion Phase (18th–19th c.)
* What was happening: This was the golden age of classical coinage, fueled by the Enlightenment and the Industrial Revolution. The systematic, classificatory nature of the era's science—seen in Linnaean taxonomy and Lavoisier's chemistry—demanded an equally systematic and universal language. Greco-Latin provided that foundation.
* Examples: oxygen, neuron, lithosphere.
3. The Synthesis Phase (early-to-mid 20th c.)
* What was happening: Science was moving beyond naming discrete physical objects (like a bone or a gas) to naming invisible forces, complex systems, and subatomic particles. This shift from the tangible to the conceptual required rearranging existing roots to create words for things that couldn't be directly observed.
* Examples: cybernetics, photon, cryogenics.
4. The Recombination & Decline Phase (late 20th–21st c.)
* What was happening: The creation of truly new words from classical roots almost completely stopped. The old roots were occasionally recombined (neuroplasticity, metagenomics), but the momentum was gone. The great age of Greco-Latin scientific coinage was over.

While this tradition faded in most fields, one area of science held on to Latin for as long as it could—but even there, a single discovery would signal the end of an era.

3. The Tipping Point: How a "Sleeping Dragon" Changed the Rules

Taxonomy, the science of classifying and naming species, was one of the last great holdouts for the classical tradition. The formal two-part Latin name for a species (like Homo sapiens) is called a binomial. For centuries, this system was the undisputed law of the land.

By the end of the 20th century, however, even this field was getting more playful. Naming conventions began to bend the rules by hybridizing classical roots with pop culture. A perfect example is Dracorex hogwartsia, a dinosaur name that literally means "Dragon King of Hogwarts." While whimsical, it still used a Latinate structure, grafting a new word onto a classical base.

But the true threshold moment—the tipping point—came with the naming of a small, sleeping dinosaur fossil: Mei long.

This name wasn't a playful bending of the rules; it was a profound rupture that broke them entirely. Why? Because it was the first accepted scientific binomial where neither element came from Latin or Greek.

Mei long (寐龙) is a direct transliteration from Mandarin meaning "sleeping dragon."

The only nod to the old system was the two-word structure. The words themselves were entirely non-classical. This wasn't just an evolution; it was a structural break. It represented the symbolic end of Latin as the unchallenged substrate of scientific naming in zoology and paleontology. A sleeping dinosaur, named in a tonal language and published in a Western journal, quietly marked the end of an empire.

The story of Mei long is a dramatic example of a much broader shift in how science names things today.

4. The New Scientific Dictionary: English, Acronyms, and Brands

So, if scientists aren't coining new words from Greek and Latin anymore, where do new terms come from? The modern scientific dictionary is built from more pragmatic and globally accessible sources.

* English Descriptives
* New concepts are often named with simple, descriptive English phrases.
* Examples: machine learning, cloud computing.
* Acronyms or Portmanteaux
* Complex ideas are shortened into memorable acronyms (words formed from the first letters of a phrase) or portmanteaux (words blended together).
* Examples: CRISPR (Clustered Regularly Interspaced Short Palindromic Repeats), LIDAR (Light Detection and Ranging).
* Branded Neologisms
* Tech companies and research groups often create catchy, brand-friendly names that are easy to market and fund.
* Examples: Bluetooth, DeepMind.

Why did this dramatic shift happen? There are five core reasons why the Classical Coinage Arc ended:

* The Rise of English: English replaced Latin as the global language of science. A term no longer needs classical roots to sound serious or be understood internationally.
* Specialization: Most new terms are created for use within a highly specialized field. They don't need to be universally intelligible across different disciplines, so a practical English name works just fine.
* Branding and Funding: New technologies and scientific ventures need catchy names that attract funding and public attention. "Gravitas" has been replaced by marketability.
* Globalization: This isn't just about diluting European prestige; it reflects a fundamental shift in the geography of science. The rise of scientific powerhouses in Asia and other parts of the world created a multilingual environment where a single classical tradition, rooted in Europe, no longer made sense as the sole linguistic foundation.
* Aesthetic Fatigue: After centuries of combining the same roots, scientists simply ran out of new, elegant-sounding combinations. The system felt tired.

So, after this journey through history, what's the big takeaway for a science student today?

5. Conclusion: A Living Language for a Living Science

Scientific language can seem like a collection of fixed, scary rules, but as we've seen, it's a dynamic system that reflects the evolution of science and culture. We've moved from an era of building new words from the bedrock of Greek and Latin to an era of rearranging old parts or creating fresh terms from practical English and modern branding.

Understanding this history can make scientific terminology feel less like a "dead language" and more like a fascinating fossil record. Each word tells a story about who was doing the science, what they valued, and how they saw the world. It’s a living dictionary for a living science, chronicling the incredible story of human discovery.

# Science Center vs. Science Museum: A Tale of Two Cultures

Introduction: More Than Just a Name

When I started my career, I was surprised to find that the science center and science museum worlds seemed utterly separate. I’d come from COSI, a place of joyful chaos, and landed at the American Museum of Natural History, which was, as I thought of it, “museum central.” This raised a question I’ve carried with me ever since: What truly is the difference between a hands-on science center and a traditional science museum? Is it just the name on the door?

The distinction lies not merely in the exhibits on display, but in their fundamental philosophies, their internal cultures, and the unique experiences they create for both the public and the staff who walk their halls. Through my time at COSI, a classic science center, and the American Museum of Natural History (AMNH), a quintessential museum, I came to understand these two different paths to scientific wonder. Our exploration starts where I did: in the gloriously unpredictable world of the science center.

1. The Science Center: A "Wild West" of Discovery (The COSI Experience)

My time as a volunteer and educator at COSI (the Center of Science and Industry) was a masterclass in the science center ethos: a culture of hands-on experimentation, informal pedagogy, and what I can only describe as controlled chaos. It was, in the best possible way, “The wild West.” This philosophy was not written on the walls; it was lived out in the daily, often unpredictable, life of the museum floor.

These moments defined the COSI culture:

* The Man Cage: One of the most dreaded volunteer posts was the simulated coal mine elevator, or the "man cage." We would pack visitors into a wooden box, press a button to make a painted canvas of rock rotate behind a grate, and try to convince them they were descending into the Earth. The true test of endurance, however, was the soundtrack: three coal-mining songs playing on an infinite, soul-searing loop. It was immersive, a little grueling, and pure science center.
* The "Wild West" Volunteer Culture: The volunteer ethos was rooted in live, visceral demonstrations. My stories from that era are a highlight reel of joyful risk: "drinking liquid nitrogen, freezing pizza for Japanese tourists... all the singed eyebrows." It was a place where the spectacle of science was performed live, with all the accompanying mishaps and singes.
* The EG Show Mishap: The Electrostatic Generator (EG) show was a classic, but it came with a critical piece of advice: put down the microphone first before creating a shock. According to volunteer lore, one educator, attempting to follow this rule, decided to hold the microphone between his legs. The resulting jolt was described as him "jumping around across the stage." It's a perfect, if painful, example of the messy, unpredictable nature of hands-on learning.

The visitor experience is designed to be one of active participation, where learning is visceral, not passive. You are invited to become part of the spectacle—to risk singed eyebrows and embrace the beautiful mess of scientific discovery, because the process itself is the point. This energetic, sometimes chaotic, culture stands in stark contrast to the more reverent atmosphere of a traditional museum.

2. The Traditional Museum: A Cathedral of Knowledge (The AMNH Experience)

If COSI was the Wild West, then the American Museum of Natural History (AMNH) was "museum central"—a cathedral built to house the artifacts and legacy of scientific inquiry. The ethos here is one of preservation, historical authority, and the institution as a sacred repository of knowledge. The weight of its history is palpable in its very architecture.

These moments illustrate the unique culture of AMNH:

* A Place of Legacy: The museum is home to the "world's largest ethnographic library" and halls that are themselves historical artifacts, forever linked to titans like Franz Boas and Margaret Mead. One coworker checked out some books from the library and discovered one of the books had what appeared to be Margaret Mead’s personal bookplate in the front. The institution doesn't just display history; it is history.
* The Ghosts of Scientists Past: The legacy of the great scientists feels so alive that it verges on the supernatural. After my coworker’s discovery, she had a conversation with a janitor about Margaret Mead, and he spoke of the long-dead anthropologist in the present tense, saying, "oh yeah, I know her… She rides this Elevator a lot." It illustrates a culture where scientific legacy is so potent it feels like a living presence. In the halls of AMNH, the giants of science are not ghosts to be feared, but colleagues just down the hall.
* A Labyrinth of Knowledge: The museum is not one building but a complex of "26 to 32 buildings," a labyrinth where offices are tucked away in back halls, upstairs laboratories, and hidden among the exhibits. Finding someone's office for the first time was "always an adventure... like unlocking a new level."

The visitor experience at a traditional museum is one of reverence and observation. You are there to learn from the context provided by meticulously curated collections, to stand in awe of authentic artifacts, and to connect with a scientific legacy that stretches back for generations. Yet despite their profoundly different methods, both of these institutions are ultimately chasing the same goal.

3. At a Glance: Two Paths to Wonder

Feature Science Center (COSI) Traditional Museum (AMNH)
Core Mission Hands-on Experience & Phenomenon-Based Discovery Preservation & Collection-Based Knowledge
Exhibit Style Interactive, Experiential, and often Chaotic Artifact-Based, Curated, and Authoritative
Staff/Volunteer Culture "Wild West," Experimental, and Performance-Focused Academic, Custodial, and Legacy-Focused
Visitor's Role Active Participant: You do the science. Observer & Learner: You witness the science.

Despite these stark differences, both institutions are built on a shared foundation: the goal of sparking wonder.

4. The Common Ground: Using the Architecture of Wonder

Ultimately, both science centers and museums strive to create "self-reinforcing connections" that ignite curiosity long after a visit ends. My favorite pedagogical technique, which worked in both worlds, was "using the architecture" itself as a teaching tool.

At both COSI and AMNH, I learned to point out the subtle wonders embedded in the buildings themselves. The entire front façade of the old COSI was made of fossiliferous limestone, and if you looked closely, you could see it was "made up of fossils through and through." Similarly, the interior of AMNH was full of architectural stone where you could find cross-sections of ancient life, "identifiable, probably to the species in some cases."

By showing this to visitors, I wasn't just teaching them about fossils. I was giving them a new way to see the world. As they walked away, they would be on the hunt for more, seeing science not just in the exhibits, but in the very stones under their feet. This shared technique reveals a common goal: to connect abstract scientific concepts to the tangible world and encourage people to see that science is, in fact, everywhere.

5. Conclusion: Different Methods, Same Mission

The cultural divide between the science center and the traditional museum is real. It is the difference between focusing on the process of science and the results of science.

* A science center like COSI celebrates the dynamic, chaotic, and playful process of experimentation and discovery.
* A traditional museum like AMNH honors the preserved, ordered, and authoritative results of generations of scientific work.

Neither approach is superior. Instead, they represent two complementary and equally vital invitations to the public. One beckons us to connect with science through play and participation, the other through reverence and reflection. Both, in their own unique ways, open a door to wonder.

# I Danced Where Latin Died: Unforgettable Stories from the Hidden History of Science

We tend to picture science as a clean, sterile affair—a series of objective facts discovered in gleaming laboratories by people in crisp white coats. But behind the published papers and polished museum displays lies a reality that is messy, surprising, and deeply human. It’s a world of chaotic holiday parties, ethically compromised night shifts, and profound discoveries made far from any formal lab. Here are a few unforgettable stories from the hidden corners of the scientific world that reveal the human heart beating under the lab coat.

The Great Age of Classical Science Naming Is Over—And Someone Danced on Its Grave

For centuries, the language of science was built on the bedrock of Greek and Latin. This "Classical Coinage Arc," as scholars call it, moved through four distinct phases: an Initiation during the Renaissance, an Expansion in the 18th and 19th centuries, a Synthesis in the early 20th century, and finally, a period of Decline. The great age of inventing new scientific terms from classical roots has essentially ended.

This grand historical shift found its perfect, chaotic footnote not in a scholarly journal, but at a secret rite of paleontological misrule. The moment pivots on the dinosaur Mei long, a name that translates to "sleeping dragon" in Mandarin. Its discovery was a threshold moment: it was the first accepted binomial nomenclature where neither the genus nor the species name derived from Latin or Greek, making it a "structurally post-Latin" name. It quietly marked the end of an era.

The celebration happened, fittingly, at the American Museum of Natural History (AMNH). During a particularly wild holiday party in the turret office of paleontologist Mark Norell—a hidden tower of classification where one could look down on the skeletons of long-extinct creatures—a young educator named Nathan McKnight found himself dancing on Norell's desk. Unaware of the desk's significance as the very place that oversaw the naming of Mei long, McKnight was simply caught up in the revelry. But Norell, the high priest of this binomial heresy, watched and smiled. He was grinning, already decades into his war on linguistic orthodoxy, probably delighted that someone had unknowingly added a little chaos to the seat of classification. As McKnight would later realize:

"I danced on the desk where Latin died."

This personal, unplanned ritual—a joyful dance in a turret overlooking the fossils—serves as the perfect, mythic end to a centuries-long chapter in scientific history.

Science's Underbelly Can Be a Grimy, Lovecraftian Nightmare

Not all science happens in the hallowed halls of places like the AMNH. For every grand museum, there are a hundred anonymous labs where the work is grimy, the pressure is immense, and the ethics are… flexible.

Picture a squalid, file-stacked former apartment in Long Island City, located directly above a Subway sandwich shop. The floor sags precariously under the weight of a massive Transmission Electron Microscope (TEM). This was an asbestos testing lab, and the graveyard shift was a journey into the surreal. The room was bathed in the eerie green glow of the TEM screen. Nearby, a plasma etcher pulsed with a beautiful blue-pink plasma behind glass, and a large vacuum machine with a bell jar blackened by years of carbon deposition would emit a loud zzzrpt straight out of a Frankenstein movie. The air was thick with the smell of acetone and old paperwork, and through the walls, from a neighboring Korean charismatic church, came a ritualistic "wailing" that started around midnight.

The work itself began to corrode the soul. The lab was held to minimal accreditation standards, and one night, the owner, "Manos," forced the signing of a falsified report, claiming a sample that had tested positive for asbestos was clean. It was a clear ethical breach in a high-pressure environment.

The escape came suddenly. After receiving a voicemail about an interview for a museum job, the lab tech quit on the spot. The final act was a tense meeting with Manos to return the keys and collect a final paycheck, an exchange that felt "like a drug deal." It was a stark look into the ethically compromised side of applied science that is rarely discussed but quietly props up entire industries.

Your Heroes Are Weirder (and More Human) Than You Think

Public figures in science often cultivate a carefully crafted persona. They are educators, communicators, and inspirations. But behind that veneer, they are just people—and sometimes, they are far weirder and more wonderfully human than their public image suggests.

Consider this moment from a staff meeting at COSI, a science center in Columbus, Ohio. As the team gathered, the door opened and in walked Bill Nye "The Science Guy." According to one staff member present, the very first words out of his mouth were, "Anybody got any cocaine?"

The room went stone-faced, except for that one staffer, who burst out laughing. That laugh broke the ice, leading to a bond over a shared love for the engineering chaos of the show Robot Wars. Notably, this staffer wasn't wearing the official green lab coat because he had been working on a project outside the museum. His boss had just chided him for it as Nye walked in.

Later, when it was time for a group photo, the boss pointed it out again: "You're not in a lab coat." Before he could defend himself, Bill Nye stepped in with a perfect, cinematic callback, using the exact phrase the staffer had used earlier: "hey he was working." The encounter ended a few minutes later with the two of them standing next to each other at the urinal, a silent, shared moment of almost-maybe parallel intentions.

This story isn't about scandal. It’s about charisma in motion. It's a glimpse of the mischief, the shared recognition of being an outsider, and the "secret outlaw brotherhood of scientific mischief" that makes these figures so much more complex and relatable than the one-dimensional heroes we see on screen.

The Most Profound Discoveries Aren't Always in a Lab

Sometimes the most important scientific work has nothing to do with a profession and everything to do with a mode of experience. The deepest insights can emerge at the boundaries of consciousness, empathy, and human connection, far from any controlled experiment.

This is a story known as "Sasha's Last Three Drops." It centers on a tiny vial of LSD, alleged to have come directly from the lab of the legendary psychedelic chemist Sasha Shulgin. The author of this story shared the final three drops with his schizophrenic ex-girlfriend. The motivation was not recreational; it was an act of what he calls "harm-reduction intimacy"—a way to ensure she didn't take all three drops herself. It was less a "trip story" and more a "guardian story."

The experience that followed was not one of cosmic bliss but of terrifying clarity. As the effects peaked, he witnessed her face transform into a "glowing mask" of "plasma," a burning, blue-white light. From her came a cry not of fear, but of profound understanding—"horror at all creation, but not fear. Just horror and clarity." The emotion was so powerful that it could only be described by the German word Wut—a deep, righteous wrath that was somehow also loving.

This guarded fall into the depths of another's consciousness was not an experiment in any traditional sense. It yielded no data, but left a permanent mark. The experience was, in his words, "vivid and unresolvable," creating a lasting sense of what he and his ex used to call patina—an "imprint of existence" that marks a thing. It’s the feeling that the entire world is a three-dimensional sticker that’s been peeled off and laid back down, but not quite right—very slightly out of alignment. It was a direct encounter with a truth that left not an answer, but a permanent shift in perception, proving that the most staggering discoveries can come from the most liminal and personal of spaces.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Science, in the end, is not a sterile collection of facts printed in a textbook. It is a profoundly human endeavor—chaotic, funny, corrupt, beautiful, and filled with unforgettable stories. It’s a dance on a desk, a signature on a false report, a joke in a conference room, and a shared moment of terrible clarity. These are the myths and legends that give science its soul.

What myths, legends, and quiet revolutions are hiding in the margins of your own world?

The Asbestos Lab Nightmare

This is a detailed, multi-part narrative of working the graveyard shift at an under-regulated asbestos testing lab in Long Island City. It is framed as "the worst job I ever had."

* The Setting: The lab was located in a former apartment above a Subway sandwich shop, next to a Korean charismatic church. The space was squalid, crammed with files, and featured a massive TEM (Transmission Electron Microscope) on a sagging floor.
* The Characters:
* Manos: The Greek, chain-smoking, and paranoid owner.
* Coworkers: A mix of "thugs with microscopes" on PLM duty, former Soviet nuclear engineers from Belarus, and a microbiologist from Michigan.
* The Work: The job involved the eerie, solitary task of analyzing air sample filters on the TEM, identified by its "eerie green glow." The work was accompanied by the "midnight wailing" from the adjacent church's prayer services.
* Ethical Crisis: The lab operated with minimal oversight, passing an annual accreditation but otherwise engaging in questionable practices. The narrator correctly identified asbestos on a test sample but was overruled. The breaking point came when Manos forced the narrator to sign a retraction for a positive asbestos finding on a real sample, blaming the sampler.
* The Escape: Having persistently pursued an outreach educator job at AMNH, the narrator received a promising voicemail and quit the lab immediately without notice. The final interaction with Manos was a tense exchange of keys for a final paycheck, where Manos sneered at the new job: "What, like playing with kids?"

Grassroots Science Communication: Sexology On Tap (SoT)

This narrative details the creation and ethos of an informal, bar-based science talk series.

* Origin: The idea for "Sexology On Tap" (SoT) was conceived during a St. Patrick's Day pub crawl with a fellow educator, Leah. It was inspired by the existing "Astronomy on Tap" series.
* Format and Vibe: SoT was a free monthly event held at The Way Station, a steampunk/geek bar in Brooklyn. It blended accessible science presentations, humor, trivia, and personal stories on topics related to sexology, human behavior, and animal anatomy. The tone was irreverent, queer-inclusive, and aimed to turn "taboo into talkable."
* Notable Details: A recurring trivia prize was a duck-shaped corkscrew, an inside joke referencing the anatomy of duck penises, which was specially ordered from the UK. The series later had a single edition in Columbus, Ohio, before being halted by the COVID-19 pandemic.

Pedagogy and Memory: The Architecture of Learning

* Application: This principle was applied by using the architectural stone of museums (like the fossiliferous limestone at COSI or the various stones at AMNH) to teach geology. By pointing out fossils in the building, it creates an opportunity for spaced repetition as visitors encounter them throughout their visit, reinforcing the lesson organically.
