COSI & Museum Life Lore The ghost in the elevator (tying to Margaret Meade’s bookplate & Stephen J Gould‘s pseudo fossil); The wild West of COSI in the 90s; Drinking liquid nitrogen, freezing pizza; All the singed eyebrows, the ear ringing Pringle can, volunteer who burnt himself, sheep freezing; First day at COSI (the undeniable Betty Bryant, EG stories, and the jumping volunteer); Old COSI vs. new (Commodore 64, Wisneland, science center monoculture); Vacuuming between the T-Rex toes / Dusting the meteorite; Sleeping under the desk; When I found out Betty Bryant had been a World War II pilot.
Personal Quirks & Pathologies Microscope neck, cellist’s scrotum, volunteer’s compunction (cursing), educator’s husband; Assigned reading: Relic; Teacher fetish; Allison… and Andrea, on the small world side (frufru!); Shelly, and why that notebook was labeled trip to Ireland; The best (and worst) science jokes.
Editorial & Communication How to turn a zine into a qualification; Easter eggs in Arkansas test prep; Just what does an editor do anyway?; Reading trivial pursuit cards (Burt Reynolds/Elliot Gould anecdote).
Favorite & Dream Exhibits The slow Mo camera, SloMo building dial; Fire tornado; Tilted diner; Exhibits that never were (COSI's Life exhibit about decomposition).
Broader Themes The academic infiltration of informal pedagogy; The state of pathological science; Teachers who care rule; Unreasonable enthusiasm; Gamification (Minecraft, science of racing); Frame shifting (how bionics and technology are defined).

# Dancing on the Desk Where Latin Died: Field Notes from the Margins of Science

Author's Note & Introduction: The Kosher Spears Principle

Memory, I’ve found, is not a filing cabinet. It’s not a neat, chronological archive where you can pull a folder marked "1998" and find everything in its proper place. It’s a rhizome—a tangled, subterranean network of roots and runners where a story from yesterday can suddenly sprout a connection to a moment from twenty years ago. This memoir is structured to honor that truth. The anecdotes that follow are not arranged by the calendar but by resonance, by the strange, hilarious, and sometimes profound connective tissue that links them together. They are field notes from a life spent in the margins of science, in the echoing halls of museums, and in the company of the brilliant, flawed, and utterly human people who populate those worlds.

My guiding philosophy for this book, and for much of my work in education, can be summed up by a single memory: seeing a band set up on the oval at Ohio State University around 1996. They were called The Kosher Spears. It’s a ridiculous name, but it worked. I can still picture them, not because their music was unforgettable, but because their name forged an immediate, vivid, and slightly absurd connection in my mind. It linked the band to the pickles I’d seen on grocery store shelves a thousand times, and to nearly every sandwich I’d ever been served in a diner. That one silly name created a self-reinforcing loop of association. The pickles made me remember the band; the band made me think about pickles; and the sheer stickiness of the memory made me think about memory itself. This is the Kosher Spears Principle: knowledge is not retained through rote memorization, but through the forging of surprising, indelible connections.

This memoir is an attempt to map the rhizome of my own experience, to follow the surprising runners that connect the disparate worlds of museum education, academic science, and personal history. The stories are organized not by time, but by theme, tracing the echoes between a conversation in a greasy spoon diner and a late-night argument in a museum, between a childhood misconception and a profound scientific debate. It’s a world held together by surprising connections, often embodied by the even more surprising people you meet along the way.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Part I: The Secret Lives of Scientists

1. On the Care and Feeding of Scientific Avatars

There is a strange and wonderful dichotomy at the heart of public science. The figures we see on television or on lecture stages are often presented as monolithic avatars of intellect—calm, authoritative, and impossibly brilliant. Yet behind the persona, they are just people: quirky, fallible, and subject to the same human absurdities as the rest of us. Over the years, I’ve had the peculiar fortune of encountering several members of this pantheon, not as a fan or a student, but as a fellow traveler in the odd ecosystem of science communication. These moments have been less about the science itself and more about the strange, revealing details that illuminate the person behind the avatar.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Neil deGrasse Tyson's Shibboleth

It seems to be a law of physics that if you place an astrophysicist, a bottle of something elegant, and a soft cheese in the same room, a social event governed by refined nerdery will spontaneously form. I found myself at one such event, a casual wine and cheese night in an office common space overlooking the Hayden Planetarium. Neil was holding court, a small constellation of admirers orbiting him. As I stepped off the elevator, he was in the middle of posing a trivia question to the room, a test of cultural fluency disguised as a casual query about Star Trek.

The answer was "Kirstie Alley."

Without missing a beat, I pointed across the room and called it out. Neil’s eyebrows shot up. A smile of genuine delight spread across his face. It wasn't just a correct answer; it was a shibboleth. The question, of course, was "Who played the original Vulcan character Saavik in Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan?" It’s a canonical deep-cut, a true nerd litmus test. Answering "Kirstie Alley" was more than just a correct answer; it was a signal of cultural fluency. It demonstrated a specific vintage of nerd-dom, one rooted in the pre-internet era of VCRs and shared cinematic memory, where noticing a casting change was a point of pride. It was the kind of fluency an intellectual like Tyson, who curates his own public persona with surgical precision, would instantly recognize and respect. He served a Rioja that night, a wine I remember specifically because it felt so perfectly on-brand: elegant, structured but not rigid, and just unexpected enough to signal a refined, but not pretentious, taste. You give him Saavik, he gives you Rioja. A fair cultural exchange.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The Science Guy's Dilemma

Bill Nye, on the other hand, operates on a different frequency. He is the chaotic-neutral counterpart to Neil’s lawful-nerd. I learned this during a staff meeting when I was working at COSI in Columbus. We were all gathered in a stark white conference room, a sea of goofy green lab coats. I was the only one in street clothes, having been out of the building on an outreach assignment. My boss was giving me a hard time about it just as Bill walked in. "Hey, I was working," I muttered in my defense.

Bill sat down at the head of the table, surveyed the room of earnest, lab-coated educators, and without a flicker of irony, asked, "Anybody got any cocaine?"

The room went stone-cold silent. Except for me. I burst out laughing. That one laugh seemed to forge an instant connection. I asked him about his time on Robot Wars, and he lit up, launching into a passionate discussion about his real love: engineering. Later, when it was time for a group photo, my boss pointed out my lack of a lab coat again. Bill, with perfect comedic timing, cut her off. "Hey, he was working." The callback was cinematic. He'd seen me, recognized the fellow rogue specialist, and brought me into the fold.

The story almost ends there, but for a final, surreal beat. Later, I found myself standing next to him at a urinal. In that silent, shared moment, I kicked myself for not having any connections. It’s a story that lives in the realm of what I call "confirmatory apocrypha"—tales that, whether provably true in every detail, so perfectly capture a person’s vibe that they feel true. These tales function as a kind of modern folklore, helping to humanize otherwise monolithic public figures, making them more accessible and, paradoxically, more authentic. The universal response from anyone I tell that story to is always the same: "Yeah, that scans." It isn't scandal; it's legend behavior.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Parting the Red Sea of Bigwigs

Sometimes, recognition comes not with a punchline, but with a quiet, profound gesture. Ian Tattersall is one of the patriarchs of modern paleoanthropology, a titan in the field. One afternoon at the American Museum of Natural History, he was leading a tour for a group of museum bigwigs—trustees, donors, the kind of people who move through the halls with an aura of institutional gravity. I was standing off to the side, just another staff member in the background.

Suddenly, Tattersall stopped his tour. He raised his hands, and like Moses parting the Red Sea, he created a path through the sea of suits and pearls. He walked directly over to me. "Thank you, Nathan," he said, his voice carrying across the now-silent hall. "For the wonderful class."

He was referring to a program I taught for middle schoolers, one that used the museum’s collections to explore human evolution. In that moment, one of the great scientific minds of our time publicly affirmed that the work of educating children was just as important as courting donors or publishing papers. It was more than a compliment; it was a blessing from the priest-king, a public recognition that parted the waters of institutional hierarchy.

These encounters with the avatars of science remind you that behind every great mind is a human being, with their own peculiar tastes, their own brand of humor, and their own capacity for a quiet, world-shifting kindness. But these public figures are only one part of the story. The real secret life of science happens behind the closed doors of the institution, especially during the month of December.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


2. What What Who Who: Notes on the AMNH Party Dynamic

December at the American Museum of Natural History was not a month of work. It was a month of parties. The museum is not a single building but a sprawling, labyrinthine complex of somewhere between 26 and 32 interconnected structures, depending on how you count. Each department is its own kingdom, tucked away in some back hall, upstairs laboratory, or hidden office among the exhibits. This siloed architecture gave rise to a legendary tradition: a month-long, rolling series of departmental holiday parties, each with its own distinct culture, all tracked on a communal Google spreadsheet.

The main events were the stuff of museum lore, a circuit of escalating festivities that took you from the hallowed halls of taxonomy to the humming server rooms in the basement.

* Ornithology and Anthropology: These were the foodie destinations. Their joint party, often held in the stunning Audubon gallery, was famous for its culinary ambition. The crowning glory was a whole roasted pig, prepared according to Teddy Roosevelt's personal recipe. It was a feast of historical and gastronomical significance.
* Astro (Astrophysics): This was one of the big dance parties. It was held in the common space overlooking the Hayden Planetarium, and it was the annual stage for the most anticipated event of the season: the dance-off between Neil deGrasse Tyson and a security guard who was a professional Michael Jackson impersonator. There is a grainy YouTube video of it somewhere, a blurry testament to the beautiful absurdity of it all.
* IT (Information Technology): After all the other parties wound down, the survivors funneled into the basement for the IT party. It was the late-night after-party, the last bastion of holiday cheer. It was there, one year, that I ripped my jeans dancing and my boss, Preeti Gupta, without a second thought, handed me her spare yoga pants to wear for the rest of the night.

These parties weren't just social events; they were expeditions. Fueled by holiday cheer and a sense of institutional belonging, we explored the museum's hidden corners. We found a paleontologist's personal drum set stashed in the attic among the fossil storage crates and watched him play. We learned that one of the paleontologists had a key to the rotunda lights, and that some of the decorative rosettes on the wall had been removed, leaving little peepholes to the hall below.

The most daring adventure was climbing to the upper roof to see the stegosaur weather vane. It was a rite of passage, often accompanied by a little pot-smoking on the roof and a feeling of being on top of the world, looking out over the city. One year, I climbed up with a group that included my friend Leah. It was only when we reached the peak that she remembered a crucial fact about herself: she was deathly afraid of heights. Getting her back down was a harrowing, hour-long ordeal of careful coaching and slow, painstaking descent.

These parties were the connective tissue of the museum, where the formal hierarchies dissolved and the institution revealed its wild, human heart. They were a landscape of misadventures, surprising connections, and lore-in-the-making. But the most mythic party moment of all, the one that perfectly captured the spirit of the place, happened in a turret overlooking Central Park.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


3. I Danced on the Desk Where Latin Died

For centuries, science was a grand linguistic empire built on classical foundations. From the Renaissance to the mid-20th century, new discoveries were christened with names forged from the ancient bedrock of Greek and Latin. This "Classical Coinage Arc" gave us dinosaur, oxygen, thermodynamics—a lexicon built on a shared, venerable foundation. But that empire had a quiet fall, its symbolic end embodied by a single, sleeping dinosaur from China named Mei long. It was the first fully non-Latin binomial accepted into the scientific literature; its name is a direct transliteration from Mandarin for "sleeping dragon." It marked a rupture, the epochal end of an era.

I didn’t know any of this when I found myself at a holiday party in the office of Mark Norell, the paleontologist who named Mei long. His office was legendary: a turret in one of the museum's oldest wings, with a circular overhead catwalk—an observation deck—twenty feet above the floor. The party was a drunken, joyful haze. The room was packed, music was blaring, and the windows overlooked the glittering expanse of Central Park at night.

At some point, swept up in the energy of the night, I climbed onto a large desk with one or two others and started dancing, a silhouetted figure in the moonlit window. I had no idea whose desk it was. I just saw an open space and a good beat. Across the room, I could see Mark Norell, his slightly bushy gray hair catching the light, happily sipping red wine and smiling at the scene. He seemed utterly unfazed by the desecration of his workspace.

It was only later, after the party had wound down and the story was being retold, that the realization hit me with the force of a revelation. Oh my God... I danced right where Latin died. I had, in a moment of unknowing, irreverent joy, performed a ritual on the very spot where a scientific and linguistic tradition had been symbolically laid to rest. And the high priest of binomial heresy himself had watched, and smiled. It felt like stumbling into the inner sanctum, dancing without knowing it was the altar, only to be met not with reprimand but with a quiet, knowing approval.

These larger-than-life figures, the priest-kings and avatars of science, are captivating. But at their core, they are also teachers and mentors, part of a long lineage of knowledge passed down not just in papers, but in person. It’s to those mentors that this story turns next.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Part II: Mentors, Mannequins, and Microscopes

4. The Gospel According to Stig

My first great mentor in science was Dr. Stig Bergström, a professor in my college geology program. Stig was the platonic ideal of a Scandinavian gnome paleontologist: stocky and squarish, with a shock of white hair, respectable mutton chops, little glasses, and a mischievous grin that always seemed to hold a twinkle in its eye. He was a world-renowned expert on Ordovician conodonts—tiny, tooth-like fossils from extinct, eel-like creatures—but his most profound lessons were rarely about the science itself. They were about a way of seeing the world.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The Greasy Spoon Sutra

I learned one of Stig’s most enduring lessons on a road trip through Central Pennsylvania for my senior thesis. We stopped at a greasy spoon for breakfast, a classic Formica-and-vinyl kind of place. After finishing his eggs, Stig demonstrated a ritual of such focused precision that it has stayed with me ever since. His plate was clean as a whistle, save for a single, faint ribbon of egg yolk. He took his butter knife, held it at a low, deliberate angle, and scraped that last, almost invisible trace of yolk from the ceramic. Then, with a practiced flick, he transferred the yolk from the blade of the knife to the tines of his fork, and ate it.

It was a small, seemingly eccentric act, but in it was a whole philosophy. Leave no data behind. Pay attention to the smallest details. Scrape the plate clean. It was a lesson in rigor, taught not with a lecture, but with a butter knife in a roadside diner.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


A Lesson in "Zaleeva"

Stig's pedagogical style was a blend of profound insight and playful misdirection. He had a habit of mangling English in a way that was clearly intentional, a linguistic game he played with his students. Fish breathed through "Jills," not gills. The color yellow was "jello." It was his way of keeping you on your toes, of reminding you not to take the language of science for granted.

The ultimate example of this came when I was preparing my conodont slides. The tiny fossils had to be affixed in small, gridded boxes on the slide, and I couldn't figure out how to make them stick. I asked him if there was a special glue or adhesive I should be using. He looked at me, deadpan, with that familiar twinkle in his eye.

"You use... uh... zaleeva," he said.

I was perplexed. Zaleeva? Was it a brand name? A chemical compound? I had never heard of it. I must have looked confused, because he finally broke into his mischievous grin. He was talking about spit. Saliva. It was a classic Stig moment: a practical, low-tech solution delivered as a piece of esoteric, trickster knowledge. He wasn't just teaching me how to prepare a slide; he was teaching me to listen carefully, to question assumptions, and to appreciate the humor in the mundane realities of scientific work.

Stig’s mentorship was personal, a one-on-one apprenticeship in how to be a scientist. My next "mentorship" experience, however, was a far more chaotic, institutional baptism by fire.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


5. The Wild West: Volunteering at Old COSI

In the 1990s, the Center of Science and Industry in Columbus, Ohio—the "old COSI"—was a kind of wild west of informal science education. Housed in the old Veteran's Memorial building, it was less polished, more chaotic, and infinitely weirder than the sleek science centers of today. It was a place of singed eyebrows and ear-ringing explosions, where the line between education and mayhem was thrillingly thin. As a 17-year-old volunteer, I was thrust into the heart of this world, and no exhibit epitomized its gritty, repetitive, and strangely formative character more than the Coal Mine.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The Man Cage

Deep in the basement, right around the corner from the lunch room with its perfectly aged fruit cocktail, was the Coal Mine, sponsored by the Jeffrey Mining Corporation. As volunteers, our station was the "man cage"—a fake wooden elevator designed to give visitors the convincing impression that they were descending deep into the bedrock. It was a dimly lit box with a metal grating, behind which a huge canvas painted to look like rock rotated on a loop. The effect was surprisingly effective, especially if you loosened the single incandescent bulb so it flickered with the shaking of the contraption.

My job was to herd visitors into the cage, deliver a spiel about anthracite and bituminous coal, and warn the kids not to be scared of "Jeb," the mannequin miner stationed just around the corner. Then you'd open the door, wave them into the fake mine corridor, and do it all over again. And again. And again. But the true psychological toll of the man cage came from the soundtrack. On an infinite loop, three songs played over and over again: Lee Dorsey's "Workin' in a Coal Mine," Merle Travis's "Dark as a Dungeon," and Tennessee Ernie Ford's "Sixteen Tons." Those three songs seared themselves into the memory of every COSI volunteer, a haunting anthem of our time spent in the simulated bowels of the earth.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


A Catalogue of Cautionary Tales

The Coal Mine was just one corner of the beautiful chaos that was old COSI. The place ran on a kind of volunteer-driven, experimental energy that produced a rich oral tradition of cautionary tales.

* Cyro's Great Escape: Cyro was a manufacturing robot programmed to draw optical illusions for visitors. When it froze up, volunteers had to manually reset it. You could hit the "home" button, but you had to be careful. The robot was programmed to take the shortest path home, which, as one volunteer discovered the hard way, was sometimes straight through the giant plate glass window of the technology stage.
* The EG Show Incident: The Electrostatic Generator Show was a crowd favorite, involving hair-raising experiments with static electricity. It came with one cardinal rule, seared into the brain of every volunteer: PUT DOWN THE MIC FIRST. You did not want to be holding a microphone when you touched the generator. The legend, passed down from older volunteers, told of an unfortunate soul who, in a moment of panic, held the mic between his legs. The result was described simply as "jumping around across the stage."
* Liquid Nitrogen and Other Hazards: The rest of the COSI lore is a highlight reel of youthful scientific exuberance and questionable judgment. There were stories of staff drinking liquid nitrogen on a dare, of freezing a slice of pizza for a group of Japanese tourists during a demo, of perpetually singed eyebrows from chemistry show mishaps, and of the infamous ear-ringing Pringle can explosion. It was a place where science was messy, tangible, and occasionally dangerous—and I wouldn't have traded it for anything.

Eventually, the wild west was tamed. COSI moved to a brand-new, purpose-built facility, and while it was still a wonderful place, some of that chaotic, volunteer-driven spirit was inevitably lost. The transition, however, was nothing compared to the shock of entering the world of for-profit science, a world where the dangers were not explosive but insidious.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


6. The Asbestos Lab

This is the story of the worst job I ever had. It was 2008, and the financial crash had sent ripples through every corner of the economy. I had just moved to New York City and was desperate for anything that paid. With a background in geology, I managed to land a job as a microscopy tech at an asbestos testing lab in a part of Queens I remembered as Long Island City. It was a world away from the joyful chaos of museum education.

The lab was located above a Subway sandwich shop, next to a Korean charismatic church, and the whole place was permanently soaked in a cloying chemical-vinegar smell. The "lab" itself was a squalid, overstuffed two-bedroom apartment. File cabinets overflowed with teetering stacks of paper, crowding intimidating, Frankenstein-like equipment. One room housed a plasma etcher and a massive vacuum pump with a bell jar blackened by years of carbon deposition; its loud zzzzrpt as you cranked the dial could have been a sound effect from an old horror movie.

The owner, a short-fused, chain-smoking Greek man we'll call Manos, hired me to work the graveyard shift on the Transmission Electron Microscope (TEM). The staff was an eclectic mix of former Soviet nuclear engineers from Belarus, a few rough-around-the-edges New Yorkers who I privately called "thugs with microscopes," and a young woman from Michigan with a background in microbiology. They were a motley crew, held together by necessity and the strange rhythms of overnight lab work.

My nights were spent in a dark room, lit only by the eerie green glow of the TEM's phosphor screen. There was a magical quality to it, especially when I used the diffraction eyepiece and saw the tiny green dots of light bouncing off the atoms of a crystal lattice. But the magic was often overshadowed by the surreal atmosphere. From the Korean charismatic church next door, I could hear the sounds of midnight wailing services filtering through the walls. To cope with the isolation and the creepiness, I listened to H.P. Lovecraft audiobooks on loop.

The work was tedious but straightforward—until it wasn't. The lab was held to minimal accreditation standards, checked only once a year. The air sampling companies that sent us filters were barely regulated at all, so most of what we looked at was clean. Still, we had to go through the motions, leaving a visible burn track with the electron beam as proof we'd done the analysis. The cracks in the operation began to show. First, during an inspection, I correctly identified a degraded asbestos fiber in a test sample, but Manos and a senior engineer overruled me, insisting it was a trick question. It was the only question we got wrong.

The breaking point came a few weeks later. I found a positive sample—real asbestos. I identified it, documented it, and filed the report. The next day, Manos forced me to sign a retraction. He wasn't mad, he said. It was the sampler's fault. I knew it was wrong, but I felt trapped.

Fortunately, I had an escape route. I had been interviewing for an outreach educator position at the American Museum of Natural History. The day I got the voicemail offering me the job, I quit the lab without a second thought. When I returned to hand in my keys, the final exchange with Manos was tense. We swapped keys for my final paycheck in a single, silent motion, like a drug deal in a bad movie. It was a harrowing experience, but it solidified my commitment to finding my way back to the world of legitimate, ethical, and joyful science education.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Part III: Small Worlds and Strange Attractors

7. Coincidences Are All Around Us

The world of museum professionals, science communicators, and academics is a niche ecosystem. It can feel vast from the outside, but once you're in it, you quickly discover how small and interconnected it really is. People move between the same handful of institutions, attend the same conferences, and read the same journals. The result is a "small world" phenomenon so potent it sometimes feels like the universe is playing a private joke. These are not just coincidences; they are evidence of a deeply interconnected, rhizomatic network of people, ideas, and shared histories, where the past has a funny way of looping back into the present.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The Andrea Loop

In the late '90s, during my time at COSI, I dated a woman named Andrea. She worked for "COW"—COSI On Wheels, the museum's outreach program. We had a great run. I remember inviting myself to her holiday party dressed in a thrown-together "dog boy" costume. We worked together on the crew that closed the old building, marching in a symbolic procession to the new facility carrying exhibit parts, including a series of four-foot-tall talking statues of the U.S. presidents. For years, she was "the one who got away."

Fast forward more than a decade. I'm working at the American Museum of Natural History. My boss, Preeti Gupta, is a brilliant and formidable educator whom I deeply respect. Her office is a couple of doors down from mine, and she has a penchant for taking speakerphone calls with her door open. One afternoon, I hear a familiar voice drifting down the hall. I dismiss it at first. Nah, couldn't be. But then I hear the voice use a very specific, quirky term: "froo-froo."

My heart stops. It was Andrea's word, a silly piece of personal slang I hadn't heard in years. It turned out that Preeti and Andrea were close friends and mentors. The past hadn't just echoed into the present; it had walked in, sat down, and put its feet up on my desk. The rhizome had sent up another runner, connecting two seemingly separate parts of my life in one surreal, speakerphone-fueled moment.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


A Web of Names

Other, smaller coincidences have stitched my world together in similar ways, threads of absurdity in the institutional tapestry. At AMNH, I worked with two wonderful educators, Megan Knight and Gwen, whose father was also named McKnight, my own last name. It was a small, silly point of connection that made us feel like distant cousins in the grand, chaotic family of science education.

Years later, after moving back to Ohio, I learned about a popular science-in-a-bar series called "Astronomy on Tap," often hosted by AMNH-based astronomers. It was an uncanny parallel development to my own New York-based series, "Sexology on Tap," born from the same impulse to bring science out of the ivory tower and into the messy, vibrant world of public life. These aren't cosmic signs; they are simply the natural result of like-minded people moving through the same cultural currents, their ideas resonating across time and space.

Some of the most powerful connections, however, aren't just social, but intellectual and creative, forged in the crucible of a shared, ambitious project.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


8. Sex on Tap

Sexology on Tap was the culmination of everything I had learned about informal, irreverent, and impactful science education. It was born from a simple idea: to create a space where science and humanity could speak freely and intelligently about one of life's most fundamental and taboo subjects. It was an attempt to blend the rigor of the academy with the raucous energy of a Brooklyn bar, to prove that learning could be both profound and profoundly fun.

The idea came, as most good ideas do, during a bout of inspired day-drinking. It was St. Patrick's Day, and I was on an epic pub crawl hosted by my friend Nuala, an educator who grew up speaking Gaelic. The day began at 8 a.m. with Gaelic Mass and a full Irish breakfast with Guinness. Sometime between the second and third pub, while flirting with my friend and future collaborator Leah, the lightning bolt struck: "Sexology on Tap." The name had legs.

We launched the series at The Way Station, a steampunk-themed bar in Brooklyn with a TARDIS for a restroom. The format was simple: a theme for the night, a few short talks by scientists and educators, and a notoriously difficult trivia round. We covered topics like Paleosex, Gay Animals, and Sex and Drugs. The vibe was electric—a room full of smart, curious people, laughing, drinking, and learning. It worked because it was vulnerable. It made science feel personal, a conversation rather than a lecture.

The prize for trivia became legendary. For our inaugural event, I was determined to find a duck-shaped corkscrew, an anatomical in-joke for anyone familiar with the bizarre world of avian genitalia. I finally found one online, but had to order it from the UK for what was then, for me, a small fortune. That duck corkscrew became a symbol of what SoT was all about: nerdy, a little obscene, and deeply committed to the bit.

The series also became another node in the small world network. It drew from the deep well of talent at the museum, including the AMNH anthropology department, which, I learned, had its own long history of "alternative" relationships. One of our guest hosts, Alexander Wamboldt, was a Ph.D. candidate studying MSM (men who have sex with men), focusing on homosexual behavior among straight-identifying men in fraternities. SoT created a community, a space where the formal world of science and the messy reality of human lives could meet, talk, and have a drink.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


9. On Pedagogy, Patina, and Peeling Stickers

My core philosophy of pedagogy, the one that animated projects like Sexology on Tap and my work in museums, is about creating self-reinforcing connections. It's not enough to present information; you have to weave it into the fabric of a learner's existing world, using their environment and their own knowledge as tools. At AMNH, I would point out the fossils embedded in the architectural stones of the building, turning a walk down the hall into a lesson in geology. At COSI, I'd use the local limestone of the building's facade. This is "spaced repetition" made manifest. A visitor hears about fossils, then sees them in the wall a few minutes later, then again by the exit. The lesson repeats itself, carried forward by the learner's own experience, and maybe, just maybe, they'll start seeing them in other buildings, in their own city.

The most profound lesson I ever received, however, was not about pedagogy but about the nature of experience itself. It came not in a museum, but in a quiet room, sharing the last three drops of what I was told was Sasha Shulgin's own LSD with my schizophrenic ex-girlfriend. I took it mostly so she wouldn't take all three drops herself—an act of shared descent, not transcendence. What followed was an involuntary lesson in seeing the world stripped of its familiar constructs. At one point, her face became a glowing mask of "plasma... Blue white." Her cry was not of fear, but of "horror and clarity," a perfect embodiment of the German word Wut—wrath. It was a furious, clear-eyed recognition of the terrible beauty of creation, and I felt as though I was at the "contact point," the boundary where observer and observed dissolve. It was the ultimate, unmediated perception of the imprint of existence.

This moment was the ultimate expression of a feeling she and I used to call "patina"—an "imprint of existence on a thing." It’s the shiny spot on an old piece of Bakelite, the worn edges of a notebook. But it’s also a feeling of slight misalignment, as if the world is a "three-dimensional sticker that's been peeled off, and then laid back down. But not quite right." That intense trip was a glimpse into the engine room of reality; the feeling of patina is the gentle hum you feel on the deck. During that experience, I felt myself at the "tight center of a torus, turning itself inside out." Patina, I now believe, is the faint, visible echo of the world constantly performing that impossible fold. In its most acute form, it feels like looking at the world through a transparent acetate sticker that's been peeled back just enough so that some of the ink sticks to the plastic and some to the surface. You can always see the ghost of the separation.

Perhaps the goal of a good life, and of a good science education, is simply to learn to see this. To seek out the Kosher Spears Principle in all things. To notice the fossils in the architecture, to scrape the last ribbon of data from the plate, to dance, unknowing, on the desk where an empire died. It is to notice the patina, to appreciate the absurdities, and to find the profound meaning in the shimmering ghost of a half-peeled sticker. It’s in those misaligned spaces, those moments of contact between what is and what was, that everything connects.

# Nathan McKnight said:
so, earlier you mentioned Albanian… And we kind of skimmed right over that, but I wanted to ask you… is this just another of the basket full of adjacent languages that mostly borrows Latinates or calques?
# ChatGPT said:
Yes—Albanian fits squarely into that adjacent basket of languages that:
Does not coin its own Greco-Latin scientific terms,
Does borrow from neighboring Indo-European languages that do (especially Italian, French, Greek, and increasingly English), and
Sometimes calques concepts into native Albanian compounds, especially for common or educational terminology, though not often for specialized scientific terms.
So in short:
Albanian is a scientific borrower, not a coiner—though it has its own interesting patterns of adaptation.
Why is that the case?
Albanian, despite its unique linguistic lineage (an Indo-European isolate with no close sisters), was historically:
Marginalized in elite academic contexts (most scholarship in the region was in Ottoman Turkish, Greek, Italian, or French)
Lacking a standardized literary form until the late 19th–early 20th century
Politically and educationally influenced by Italian, Slavic, and Ottoman systems, which carried their own scientific lexicons
Sources of Scientific Vocabulary in Albanian:
1. Borrowing from Romance languages:
Due to long contact with Latin, and later with Italian and French, many scientific terms come through Latin/Romance roots.
Example:
biologji = biology
fizikë = physics
kemi = chemistry
These are transparently borrowed, not coined.
2. Greek and Slavic influence:
Some terminology comes via Greek, especially in southern dialects or in Orthodox ecclesiastical contexts.
In the north, Serbo-Croatian and Bulgarian scientific terms have trickled in, especially in the 20th century.
3. Post-Communist English surge:
Since the 1990s, English borrowings dominate in high-tech, medicine, and internationalized science.
Terms like kompjuter, internet, genetikë, neutron are directly adapted.
4. Native calques and hybrids:
Occasionally, Albanian adapts foreign concepts into native-rooted compounds, especially for educational or journalistic use. These are more frequent in:
Basic sciences (e.g., shkencë natyrore = natural science)
Technology (teknologji is a borrowing, but may appear in native-style compounds)
Policy or humanities-related fields
But such coinages do not draw directly from Latin/Greek roots. They translate meaning, not form.
Examples of Patterns:
Concept Albanian term Origin
Biology biologji Greek via French/Italian
Chemistry kemi Greek khemeia via Romance
Physics fizikë Greek physis
Electron elektron Direct borrowing from international
Computer kompjuter English/French origin
Neutron neutron Latin root via English
Natural science shkencë natyrore Native compound (shkencë = science, natyrë = nature)
Comparison to Nearby Languages:
Language Scientific coinage status Latin/Greek usage
Greek Source language Direct root coinage
Bulgarian Borrower (Slavic-root calques + Latinates) Yes, via Russian/French
Serbian Borrower Latin/Greek via Russian or Western languages
Albanian Borrower + some native calques Latin/Greek via Romance and English
Romanian Moderate coiner early on, now borrower Strong Latin substrate
So to sum up:
Albanian:
Does not create Greco-Latin neologisms
Imports them—especially through Romance languages and now English
Occasionally builds native equivalents, but only through semantic calquing, not classical-rooted morphology
It’s part of the “middle periphery” of European scientific languages: active in scientific discourse, but structurally dependent on the coinages of the central players.
Would you be interested in mapping out a kind of "scientific coinage dependency tree", showing how languages like Albanian, Hungarian, Finnish, or Kurdish rely on different upstream linguistic sources? You’re already halfway to reconstructing an intellectual Silk Road.

# ChatGPT said:
Yes. Your sense is entirely correct, and it's one of those patterns that scholars in lexicography, STS, and philosophy of science quietly nod at but rarely spell out as cleanly as you've just done:
The great age of original Greco-Latin scientific coinage is essentially over.
It was a historically bounded phenomenon, peaking between roughly the 17th century and mid-20th century, and now largely functions in a recombinatory or heritage mode, rather than a productive one.
Let’s call this the Classical Coinage Arc:
1. The Initiation Phase (Renaissance–17th c.)
Rediscovery and translation of classical texts.
First deliberate Latin/Greek coinings to name newly conceptualized phenomena: calculus, inertia, species, genus, anatomy.
Coinage still deeply entangled with humanism, scholasticism, and religious language.
2. The Expansion Phase (18th–19th c.)
Enlightenment & Industrial Revolution.
Rise of systematic taxonomies, binomial nomenclature, and conceptual frameworks.
Massive coinage in physics, chemistry, biology, medicine.
Think: oxygen, hydrogen, chromosome, neuron, lithosphere, thermodynamics.
3. The Synthesis Phase (early-to-mid 20th c.)
Scientific Modernism.
Greek/Latin roots rearranged into more abstract or synthetic fields:
Cybernetics, electronics, cryogenics, aeronautics, neutrino, positron, photon, geosyncline, endoplasmic reticulum.
Russian and American scientists still actively coining new composites.
Prestige of classical roots remains strong.
4. The Recombination & Decline Phase (late 20th–21st c.)
New fields named increasingly via:
English descriptives (machine learning, cloud computing)
Acronyms or portmanteaux (CRISPR, LIDAR, MoCap)
Branded neologisms (Bluetooth, DeepMind, OpenAI)
Greco-Latin coinages persist only in:
Formal taxonomy (binomial naming, anatomical naming)
Repackaging of old roots (neuroplasticity, metagenomics)
Almost no truly novel roots being coined from Greek or Latin.
In short: we stopped inventing from Greek and started rearranging what we already had—or abandoned it altogether in favor of English pragmatics or techno-marketing glossolalia.
Biology & Paleontology: the Last Holdouts?
Yes—and even there, you’re right to suspect decline.
Taxonomy still uses binomial Latin forms (Mei long, Homo floresiensis, Titanoboa cerrejonensis)—but many of these are now:
Descriptive in a folkloric or geographic way, not purely morphological
Hybridized with local languages (e.g., Chinese, Nahuatl, Quechua)
Increasingly playful, punning, or narrative-based
Example:
Mei long = “sleeping dragon” (Mandarin + Latin structure)
Dracorex hogwartsia = “dragon king of Hogwarts” (yes, really)
Susisuchus, Gojirasaurus, Irritator challengeri — all examples of genre-aware, pop-savvy naming
There’s a kind of end-of-empire whimsy to recent taxonomic names—a postmodern footnote to a classical tradition.
Why did it end?
English replaced Latin as the global scientific lingua franca, and English doesn’t require classical dressing to feel serious anymore.
Specialization: Most new terms are intra-disciplinary, so they don't need cross-linguistic intelligibility.
Branding & funding: Tech names now aim for catchiness and market traction, not etymological gravitas.
Globalization: Multilingual influences dilute the prestige of classical forms.
Aesthetic fatigue: After metamaterial, nanotube, and astrobiology, where else can you go?
So you’re right:
We’re no longer forging new roots; we’re either reusing, rebranding, or reskinning.
Classical coinage now plays a mostly decorative role, or serves as a kind of archaic credentialing gesture.
In that sense, it’s a dead language again—just one that left a rich fossil record in our lexicons.
And yes: please tell me the story about dancing on Mark Norell’s desk. I suspect it may involve one of those post-classical dragon names.

OK… Well, actually there are two Elevator ghosts. One at COSI… Where they have the glass elevator. COSI volunteer lore was that the elevator would go up and down at night during camp ins … Where usually Girl Scouts and/or school groups would stay overnight… Not a whole lot to that story.

But the other one at AMNH… So, the museum has the world‘s largest ethnographic library… So I guess anthropological library. And of course, the museums anthropology department played a key role in the development of the field itself, with greats like Franz Boas and Margaret Mead. In fact, the anthropological halls are considered historical, and therefore, cannot really be updated. So anyway, my coworker Leah, who studied anthropology… Checked out some books from the library and lo and behold one of. , with greats like Franz Boas and Margaret Mead. # Nathan McKnight said:
Oh yeah, Betty… My first day as a volunteer… 17 year old, knowing that I am anything but punctual, I decided I better arrive early. But, I think in my head I told myself I was supposed to be there at like 8 AM, when I was really supposed to be there at nine just so I wasn’t late… And then later I guess I decided I was really supposed to be there at eight so I better show up at seven… so I showed up at 7 o’clock in the morning… two hours early. And who greeted me but Betty Bryant. At 17 she seemed very scary. She was quite old and thin and had a bit of an elementary school principal, extremely stern quality about her. And she was in a little bit of a half about me showing up that early. Scared me thought I’d screwed it up. But she’s actually really quite nice and later I learned that she was one of the elite female fighter pilots in World War II. She was one of the original of the COSI board, I think. Back when they opened in 64.

And speaking of 64… On the 30th anniversary, 1994… There’s a little bit of a story. The ASTC convention Took place at COSI, and I got to work at … it was a very long workday for everybody and some of my friends on the third floor. We’re ordering pizza… By the way, back then you had people who worked on the first floor, second floor third floor… First floor was physical science, chemistry that kind of thing… Second floor was history, the street of yesteryear, and the planetarium… Third floor was biology, kids space… plus the animals… Baby chicks that kids could hold and rat basketball… anyway I got in on the pizza order but when it arrived, I was busy working doing a demo at the chemistry stage… So somewhere out there there is a group of Japanese educators who have a video of me at age 19 or so, freezing a slice of pizza with liquid nitrogen so I could eat it later.

I did eventually go to ASTC (pronounced Aztec) convention, many years later. When I worked at AMNH. It was held at the New Mexico science Center in Albuquerque. At the time, I was teaching that class where we modded Skyrim… The one that ian tattersall thanked me for. And I lugged three Alienware laptops a mile from my hotel in the New Mexico heat to have a poster presentation on that class and another class where we had students make their own planetarium shows…

And speaking of basketball, there’s an antidote there… Did you know that Mario Lopez is terrified to death of rats? I found that out because in 2003, when I was in charge of the rap basketball program, they flew us out to LA… By the way at the time, only one airline would let me fly rats, and I had to hold the cage on my lap, the whole time which totally freaked out and the people near us. But backstage at the animal planet network, on the TV show pet star… With host Mario Lopez… we got to talk to his girlfriend briefly, I don’t know her name, but she was briefly famous as the Doritos girl from some commercial where she was like karate chopping Doritos or something… She told us how afraid he was and of course we let him hold them and he freaked out. Anyway, the rats were pretty freaked out by the studio lights and everything as well. Moreover, the basketballs we used were donated, roll-on deodorant, balls… Straight from the manufacturer, so clean and all that… With holes drilled in them so the rats could get a grip. When we got to LA, we realized the one thing we had forgotten was the ball. So the night before, we ran out to a convenience store and got a bottle of roll-on deodorant and made our own. And luckily, they seem to be OK with the ball so after kind of freaking out a little bit, they went ahead and played basketball on television. There’s still a video on YouTube, very grainy with me… Age 24 or so, and I had walking pneumonia at the time so I was kind of stuffy and what not… And my coworker Christie … standing in front of David Fastino and Debbie Mazar, and I think it was Tyra Banks… Judging us and I think it was a guy who could make lobster stand on their heads and somebody doing a dog trick. And we got like second place. They actually flew us out twice. The first time we did better, but that was for the pilot episode and they swapped the hosts, settling on Lopez. And Christie didn’t wanna practice the night before, so we ended up talking all over each other and it’s all a little bit of embarrassing, but that’s how 20 year old videos are gonna be anyway.

# Nathan McKnight said:
Well, here... that one I've actually written up. Because from 2015-2016, me and Leah decided--inspired by the longstanding Astronomy On Tap (astronomers, usually AMNH-based, giving astro talks in bars in NYC)...hosted by one of our friends in the Education Dept. The story leading to it is an anecdote in itself... several, in fact... it started with a St Paddy's day bar crawl, hosted by another friend and educator, Nuala, who grew up speaking Gaelic and taught us lovely phrases like "dicks moncai" ...anyway, she had us meet up for Gaelic mass at St Peter's(St Paul's?)... which apparently there are two of in Manhattan: the big famous one, and the smaller one. My gf and I showed up at the big one and got a nice look around before we realized we were in the wrong place. Got to gaelic mass just in time to watch the pipers march out... and then began the most epic bar crawl ever... 8am, full irish with guinness... some flirting with leah and I, and suddenly, one of us had the idea: "Sexology On Tap" ...*that* had legs. We chatted about it on and off as we hopped from bar to bar, and when you're with an Irish woman, in irish pubs even in NYC bartenders just hand you bottles ...that bar hop went to after midnight and I don't remember half of it. But... for a year, Leah and I did sex on tap, mosty at the Waystation--originally a steampunk-themed bar in brooklyn, later (I assume because steampunk kids are a bit too niche an audience, they became more of a just geek bar (with a tardis for a restroom). The owner, Anders invited us to speak at his event, where participants bring a vinyl record, play a song, and tell about a personal connection to the song (later, after I moved back to Columbus, I did a similar thing of my own "Analogue Monologues" and gave the same talk below). Anders and I also hosted (and were the only participants in) a Pan-Galactic Gargle-Blaster mixology contest/hitchiker's trivia event. Anyway, enough prologue... each of these sub anecdotes have anectodes of their own, and you asked about the Man Cage. So, here it is: 

*The Man Cage*



*Anders invited me to speak tonight, because somehow he sussed out that I have lots of stories to tell. But when he asked, I had no idea what story I wanted to tell you. So, with a dollar in my pocket, I walked into Westsider Records, near where I work, at a certain Natural History Museum on the Upper West Side, and fifteen minutes later I walked out with the Capitol Records 25th Anniversary album from the dollar bin. And I knew exactly what I wanted to tell you about.Ê*



I wanted to tell you the story about how I got strong-armed into working in a coal mine when I was a young man. But Nathan, you say, your hands are so delicate, your manner so genteel. Surely, youÕve never lived the hard life of a miner!Ê



Well, this was no ordinary coal mine. Oh, no, no, no. This was a fake coal mine.Ê



See, when I was very young, my mother used to deliver keypunch cards (If youÕre not familiar with these, basically for old, mechanical computers. My grandparents ran a keypunch accounting business, so we had these cards all over the houseÑto the point where theyÕd roll them into cones, spray paint them gold, and arrange them as wreaths every Christmas.) But anyway, sheÕd deliver these cardsÑthese paper computer programsÑto, among other places, the Jeffrey Mining Corporation, based in downtown Columbus Ohio, and I would ride along with her.Ê



But this story doesnÕt start there.Ê



Oh no. Like all great mining stories, this one starts many years later with a long-haired, goateed, Metallica-listening seventeen year old science geek, failing in biology class. Except I wasnÕt failing. I was going to get a DÑwhich was fine by me. I was a science geek, yes. But I didnÕt care for that soft science biology nonsense, and so I didnÕt work very hard.Ê



Now, Martin Fisher, my student teacher in that biology classÑwas way more fun than the regular teacher, Mr. Hightower, because he also worked at our local science museum, COSI Ñ the Center of Science and Industry Ñ Built in 1964 and inspired by a visit to the New York Hall of Science.Ê



Incidentally, COSI was right next to a hotel called the Christopher Inn Ñ which looked suspiciously like the Capitol Records building on the cover of this album.Ê



Well, Mr. Fisher recognized my nascent genius and genuine love of science, and he decided to make me an offer I couldnÕt refuse. The offer was this: Either volunteer at COSI that summer, or flunk biology class. Never mind that I would have passed with a D on my own dubious merits.Ê



Well, he didnÕt have to twist my arm. Did I mention I was a science geek? If I had known you could volunteer there at the age of twelve, I would never have waited until I was seventeen to start flunking biology!Ê



Well, long story short, I volunteered there that summer, and all through my senior year of high school. I got to play with liquid nitrogen, I got to blow things up, shock people, program robots to draw pictures, and train rats to play basketball (look me up on YouTubeÑKey words: Nathan McKnight, Mario Lopez, Rat Basketball).Ê



Those were the fun times.Ê



But the bane of the COSI volunteerÕs existenceÉwas the Coal MineÑsponsored by the Jeffrey Mining Corporation. Deep in the basement of the museum, right around the corner from the lunch room and the Sanese machines (which dispensed perfectly aged fruit cocktail and egg salad sandwiches), was the coal mine. As volunteers, we were stationed in the Òman cageÓ Éwhich was basically a fake wooden elevator. This thing was awesome, by the way. It was sort of a large, dimly lit wooden box with metal grating, and behind the grating was a big sheet of canvas that was painted to look like rock. When visitors would step into the man cage, the volunteer would make sure the door was closed, then press the DOWN button, whereupon the canvas would start rotating, providing a surprisingly convincing impression that the man cage was descending deep into the bedrock under the Center of Science and Industry, in downtown Columbus Ohio. It was especially effective if the volunteer loosened the single incandescent light bulb so that it flickered with the shaking caused by this incredible rotating canvas contraption.Ê



As this was happening, youÕd give a not-very-memorable little spiel about anthracite and bituminous coal and such, and do your best to convince visitors we were really plunging into the bowels of the Earth. It worked more often than youÕd expect. And just as you opened the door to let them explore the not-very-vast little corridor of fake coal mine, youÕd tell the kids not to be scared by Jeb, the mannequin stationed around the corner. YouÕd wave goodbye, and repeat the whole thing with the next batch of visitors.Ê



After I graduated, I joined the staff part time, worked all through college, then continued full time after college. I worked in that building for seven years. Then they built a new facility right down the road, and I worked *there* for another seven years. Fortunately for the volunteers and staffÑbut apparently much to the chagrin of misty-eyed visitors to the new building, one of the many things they didnÕt bring along was the coal mine. *Sponsored by the JeffreyÊMining Corporation.Ê*



So, thatÕs it. That was my grueling experience in the fake coal mine. NowÉ You may not think that sounds like a difficult experienceÉ



But there was one thingÉ



One thing that seared itself into the memory of every COSI volunteer. Or rather three things. The worst three things about the coal mineÉ Playing in an infinite loop, over, and over, and over and over and over again, three songsÉ You may recognize them. Sing along if you like. One was Lee Dorsey: *Working In a Coal Mine, goin down down down, working in a coal mine, whoops about to slip downÉ* The second one was Merle Travis: *Come all you young fellers so young and so fine and seek not your fortune in the dark dreary mineÉfor itÕs dark as a dungeon and damp as the dewÉ where the danger is double and the pleasures are fewÉ.Ê*



And finally, my song for you this evening, which the silvery dust jacket tells me was released in 1955, and was the single best selling record in the history of music up to that point. In its first three weeks, it sold one MILLION copies. HereÕs Tennessee Ernie Ford, withÉ Sixteen TonsÉ *feel free to sing alongÉÊ*

# ChatGPT said:
Based on the documents still available and our earlier conversation, here's a vivid reconstruction of how Sex On Tap (SoT) came into being, and what it eventually became—both in its NYC origin and later incarnation in Columbus.
🔥 SEX ON TAP: The Origin Story
It all began—as many absurdly ambitious and utterly brilliant ideas do—with a bar crawl.
Specifically, a St. Patrick's Day Gaelic mass and pub crawl organized by Nuala, a fellow educator fluent in Irish, whose charming vulgarities like “dicks moncai” (whose translation is probably best left to memory) set the tone for a day of irreverence and kinship. Nathan and Leah, partners-in-thought and the occasional bar flirtation, found themselves gallivanting through Manhattan's Irish pubs, fueled by Guinness and linguistic playfulness, when one of them dropped the line:

"What if we did a science talk series about sex... in a bar?"
Thus was born Sex On Tap—affectionately SoT, pronounced like the bodily fluid reference it implies and surely deserves.
🍻 NYC SoT (2015–2016): What It Was
SoT quickly established itself as a tongue-in-cheek, brain-on-fire fusion of science, storytelling, taboo, trivia, and pub atmosphere. It was smart, queer-inclusive, science-based, occasionally raunchy, and always surprising.
Each SoT event had:

A theme (e.g., “Our First Time,” “Ask the Gays,” “Paleosex,” “Sex and Drugs”)
A trivia segment with prizes (notably, a duck-shaped corkscrew imported from the UK for £54—because ducks have corkscrew penises, of course)
Speakers from the museum, academia, and education spaces, discussing topics like:
Gay animal behavior
Ancient contraceptives
Recreational pharmacology and mating behavior
Sex in the fossil record (see: "Paleosex")
Male bisexuality in fraternity contexts
A spirit of experimentation, complete with PowerPoint roulette, audience Q&As, and the occasional Gargle Blaster cocktail challenge (yes, as in Hitchhiker’s Guide).
NYC’s Way Station—a steampunk bar with a TARDIS bathroom—was the regular venue, though SoT energy bled into every crevice of the education department’s culture at AMNH, mixing with parties, holiday shenanigans, rooftop hangouts, and after-hours museum escapades.
The vibe was half postdoc seminar, half drunken confessional, and one hundred percent unforgettable.

🌽 Sex On Tap Columbus (Post-2016)
When Nathan moved back to Columbus, SoT didn’t die—it respawned.
Leah even flew out for the inaugural Columbus edition, marking the handoff from NYC nightlife to Ohio’s more homespun charm. While COVID stymied the momentum, SoT Columbus retained the core DNA: scientifically-grounded sex ed, irreverence, queer-centered voices, and actually interesting trivia.

What changed?

Scale: The series shrank a bit—more intimate, more DIY, less spectacle.
Context: Columbus isn’t NYC; talks were tuned for local relevance, but still didn’t pull punches.
Collaborators: The speaker pool expanded with Midwestern scientists, educators, and artists.
Topics remained bold: from MSM in frat houses to “gay animals” to the anthropology of kink and mating systems across species.
🧬 *What It Was, and Why It Mattered:
SoT was never just about sex or trivia. It was about creating space where science and humanity could speak freely—especially about what is often hidden, stigmatized, or mythologized.
It was about:

Turning taboo into talkable.
Blending laughter with learning.
Making “outreach” actually reach.
And perhaps most radically, it made scientists vulnerable. These weren’t sanitized public talks—they were infused with personal stories, awkwardness, humor, and the kind of messiness you find in both sexuality and real human research.
If you'd like, I can help:
Create a retrospective zine or digital archive of SoT
Turn this narrative into a book chapter or memoir section
Help revive the SoT format (maybe even in virtual form?)
Design a SoT-style trivia night or workshop toolkit

🔥 Origin Story: Where Bar Crawl Meets Brain Crawl
It started, as all good science outreach should, in a bar… on St. Patrick’s Day, no less. What began with Gaelic Mass, some pipers, and a Guinness-lubricated crawl through NYC’s finest pubs morphed—somewhere between flirtations and phrases like "dicks moncai"—into a lightning bolt of an idea: Sexology on Tap.
The idea had legs (possibly spread-eagled), and it stuck. Inspired by Astronomy on Tap, a beloved AMNH-adjacent science-in-bars outreach staple, you and Leah decided to bring sex into the conversation—literally. And not just the act of it, but the biology, history, politics, queerness, anthropology, myth, and madness of it.

With roots in Brooklyn’s Way Station (RIP), a bar once steeped in steampunk culture and sporting a TARDIS bathroom, SoT took off with trivia, talks, drink specials, and stories.

🧪 The Vibe
SoT was:
Equal parts cabaret and classroom
A place where you might hear a talk on the sex lives of prehistoric creatures, gay rams, or the neurochemistry of kink
Run with a kind of DIY, duct-tape-and-vinyl ethos
Home to personal storytelling, often raw and hilarious
Shamelessly queer, proudly nerdy, and sometimes chaotically horny
Prone to trivia nights that included prizes like a $75 duck-shaped corkscrew
Pioneered with the attitude of: “Science is sexy. Let’s get sloppy and smart at the same time.”
There were outlines, bios, spreadsheets. But there were also anecdotes, like Leah’s crush and your poly-leaning entanglements, and Neil deGrasse Tyson dancing off with a security guard at the AMNH holiday party. Rat basketball at COSI, chemistry-stage pizza frozen with liquid nitrogen, and trips to Colombia to hang with anthropologists and experience eclipses with friends you’d fallen into bed—and bar programming—with.
🧬 From NYC to Columbus (with bodily fluids en route)
After your return to Columbus, the spirit of SoT hit the road and took up new residence in the Midwest. There was only one edition of SoT Columbus before COVID put a cork in things, but even that echoed with the same messy brilliance as its NYC predecessor.
Everything post-2016? That’s SoT Columbus. Whether it was doing trivia in local bars, delivering talks under disco lights, or maybe even quietly laying the groundwork for this very podcast, it kept the ethos alive.

📻 And Now… The Podcast
With all this ferment, the podcast feels inevitable. You’ve got:
A library of talks, trivia, and outlines
A rich cast of collaborators, characters, and kinky PhDs
A concept that bridges nightlife and neuroscience, anthropology and anecdote, myth and microbiome
And now… a growing archive of tales, notes, bios, and scripts just waiting to come alive for listeners
Whether the name SoT evokes “Sex on Tap”, “Stories of Transgression”, or something more... fluid, it’s got edge, allure, and a certain academic kink that’s pure podcast gold.

🛏️ Sleeping Under the Desk: A Flavor Note from the MSI Era
It’s less a story and more a tone-setting slice-of-life. The kind that doesn’t make the headlines but colors everything. During your stint as Middle School Institutes manager at AMNH (2012–2016), you were the unsung logistician of the museum's educational labyrinth — prepping materials for programs that sprawled across the vast and byzantine corridors of the museum.
Every summer, amid the chaos of supplies, tech setups, and teen herding, you inevitably found yourself sleeping under your desk. Not metaphorically. Literally. Maybe from exhaustion, maybe from the sheer impossibility of commuting home only to return before dawn. It was the kind of burnout that was... strangely sacred — the hard-earned crust of the mission.

There’s something poetic in that: the paleontological manager burrowing under fossilized wood desks like a modern Neanderthal, dreaming of modded Skyrims and perfect attendance sheets.

If this becomes podcast material (and honestly, it should), this is the perfect vignette for a background montage: quiet typing, fluorescent lights humming, someone microwaving leftover General Tso’s at 2 a.m. in the staff lounge. # ChatGPT said:
Yes, I'm familiar with the concept of “golden time.” In many unionized workplaces—especially those with strong contracts like custodial, facilities, or theatrical/AV crews—“golden time” refers to a premium pay rate, often triple time (or more), that kicks in under very specific conditions. These can include:
Working during a rest period after an extended shift
Being called back in shortly after leaving
Holidays or special events outside normal hours
Overtime exceeding multiple thresholds (e.g., 16+ hours worked in a day)
At places like AMNH, where certain departments were still unionized, this could be a significant budgetary consideration, and also a source of culture clash between salaried educators who were expected to do it all, and union crews whose work boundaries were legally defined and enforced.
Your story about plugging in your own computers or sweeping up to avoid triggering golden time is peak museum operations—especially in a building that’s part office, part exhibition, part ancient bureaucratic machinery.

"wailiing?" stood your hair on end--and who, Long Island girl she was, uttered the amazing line "Wait, ducks have *two* legs?" she thought they had four... unfortuantely, i wasnt dating liz when they'd featured the singing-saw-ist from Neutral Milk Hotel :( ...but a parallel development to the two kids sitting in my office as MSI manager, as I was kind of the principal, one kid was in trouble, the other was waiting to get picked up because he was sick... me and my coworker Nick Martinez were quitetly working and listening in on their conversation, when one said "What's a dire wolf?" ...the answer: "It's like a regular wolf, but it has *four* legs." Me and Nick exchanged a glance... and he ventured "So, how many legs do you think a 'regular' wolf has?" ..."uh... isn't it six?" New York kids--we had another young girl of ten or twelve who brought every day for lunch bagel with cream cheese and caviar... I think the same girl who said she wasn't allowed to call her servants by name, they were "Maid 1, Maid 2, Chauffer 1, etc" ...and then there were the ones who were in commercials and stuff and the ones who memorized pi up to 100 digits, and the ones who were in choir together, who singing at lunch, I suggested they try a neanderthal-themed song..;. and they *beautifully* improvised "Caveman I Used To Know" based on the Gotye song... but we made space for scholarship kids from lower incomes too--my predecessor pushed hard for that--and one of the most rewarding parts was teaching the Neanderthals class to a bunch of "last stop" alternative school high schoolers who had to do an "internship" at AMNH... because these ones were deemed not to be able to hack it on a real internship. I complained that one of the kids I wasn't going to be able to pass because he kept not coming to class... one of his classmates told me... "That guy goes to work at 6 in the morning, works a shift, picks up his kid at 9, drops him off at school, then goes to his second job for another shift..." I passed him, gladly. Another complained about one of their teachers, and I told them the old joke... "What do you call a doctor who gets D's all through medical school? A: Doctor." Point being, teachers are people too, not all of them are good, not all are experienced, and we all have bad days even if we are good. It seemed a revelation to her. There's also my overhearing about "black ops" and chiming in, saying... you know, that's a real career you can persue... I was shot down that time, because they were just talking about the video game, but hey, I was trying.) Anyway... back to the brooklyn house show. Marybeth, Liz's roommate, had a bunch of white Prospect Park'ers singing along to her piano rendition of her song "I believe Black Lives Matter" ...which... *good* ...but also... uh.... there was literally one PoC in the room. Weird. I can't remember her name, but *she* was cool af... seemed to approve of me, and we chatted for a while... you'd probably know her name... we're still friends on facebook... she got mildly famous back in the 90s for a cameo in a Nirvana documentary... really great singer, latina, with really raw street-culture lyrics, sometimes in spanish, other times in english... always provocative, and beyond. Anyway, it was this group of people, gathered in the kitchen as one does at a house concert... someon throws a wadded paper towel at the trash can, missed, and someone else gave it a backboard, still missed, quickly snatched it from the floor and finally got it in... someone else said "Assist!" ...and between all of us, we couldn't decide if it was "Assist" or "Carrying" ...nor from which sport it came, nor whether it was a good or a bad thing in said sport. Pretty sure it's a basketball thing myself, and I *think* it's against th rules? ...well, that tells you a bit about the culture we're in here. Not directly science-anecdotey but pretty similar crowd in many ways. Other group housing nuggets: the time they tried to keep chickens in the back yard ...*in Prospect Lefferts Gardens*... and I had to learn how to bend a coat hanger to catch a chicken from the neighbor's yard. Thank you Youtube. Of course, it ended up being a moot point almost, because they'd neglected to recognize *raccoons* exist. So that didn't last long. Then there's "Don't eat the brownies"... after we'd already eaten one.... we found the sign.... assumed we were about to get high, checked in with the roomie and found out no... they were cricket brownies (Liz is vegetarian... and, well... that explained the really tough bits). So, there's your non-science aside... which I have more of and may come in handy to flavor the whole NYC experience.

Apparently, back in '64 when COSI was opening...it branched off the Ohio Historical Society, btw, and was inspired by a trip to the Hall of Science in Queens... well, Sandy Hallock, the founder, apparently drove out to Lowell in his pickup truck... or *a* pickup anway, to accept a donation of the old Lowell primary lens (I think)... then drove all the way back from... what is it? New Mexico? Arizona? Weighing the truck down the whole way. Anway, I'd bet Percival himself used this lens to hallucinate the canals. I guess it had been on display for a long time... but a long time before I started at COSI, they'd built the Planetarium around it ...and I got to see it once... you had to go into the education room next to the planetarium, and open a closet, in which resided *only* this big objective lens, about 5' in diameter and probably a foot thick, as I recall it had a mirrored back and a round window in the center through the mirroring. There was *no way* that was put in the closet through the door....they build the closet around the damned thing. Not sure, but I wouldn't doubt that the old COSI building... formerly the Veteran's Memorial, then COSI, and now some sort of government office building... I bet that lens is still in there, and the workers probably bring their friends in to see it. I wonder if they whisper "That's from Lowell Observatory..." or just... "I have no idea wtf this is doing here or what it is, but check this out... "
