Let’s go with this one from your list:

"Using the architecture (pedagogy: creating connections, creating self-reinforcing connections, the rocks of AMNH, the fossils of COSI)"

Wanna walk me through this one? Sounds like it’s rich with physical space, learning philosophy, and layered memory.

# Nathan McKnight said:
Well, one of the things I’ve learned over the years about pedagogy is that you’ve got to use the limited time that you have with a student or a learner or whatever you wanna call them wisely… Particularly in the museum, education environment, where unlike a classroom teacher who has the same students Typically for a whole year or most of the year, or even multiple years sometimes… You are there with a group of people who may only be there for that one half an hour show or Florida demonstration that could last anywhere from a couple seconds to 10 minutes or 20 minutes even… So, if you wanna make an impact, one of the best ways to do, it is to connect to things that your audience already knows… Which sounds obvious and is in fact… But it’s surprising how often that concept is neglected. Typically, when educators, especially people who have not been trained in education, I would say… Or people who don’t have not spent a long time doing and thinking about education… Normally, explicit connections to everyday life, and things like that are done by way of example… That is, to explain something that might otherwise be harder to explain and specifically for that purpose. You’re making a metaphor to make something hard to understand. Easier to understand… which, of course, is valuable things to do but, there are a lot more reasons to connect to daily life. Or to connect to other knowledge areas that people have. For one, if you activate one knowledge area, then it will tend to activate adjacent knowledge areas so, you’re more likely to remember information if it is connected, no matter how loosely… But ideally more vividly… No matter how loosely it’s connected to some other thing that you already know. You’ve increased the chances that both, or however, many brain regions are activated get reactivated. And of course, you can be smart about what you connect things to… Honestly, The first time I think I recognize this was in college around 1996 or so, seeing a band set up on the oval Ohio State University… They were called the Kosher Spears. It didn’t hurt that they were good, but I don’t think I would remember the name to this day if they weren’t named after a kind of pickle that I’ve seen on the shelf hundreds of times and have received with almost every restaurant sandwich I’ve ever bought… That band that probably hasn’t been together for 20 years maybe only even perform that one time… is indelibly stuck in my mind. At least their name is and, by association, some memory of the actual performance. And moreover, because I connected that concept to pedagogy, which I was already working part-time at COSI and so had a reason to think about these things… That continued the association and so by being connected these three things, the band, the pickles And the paregoric use of connection all carried forward into the present day for me and has become an explicit part of the way I teach. Now, again the smarter you are about the associations you try to make, the more impact and more nuance impact you can have.

So, a concept that I think usually I hear associated with language learning… Space repetition I believe is what it’s called. You remember things better particularly vocabulary, but I’m sure other things as well if you hear them repeated or making those associations at regular intervals at wider and wider spaces so, repeat a word five or 10 times today 3 to 5 times tomorrow, once the next day and the next day and the next day… And then once we can show on and that’s kind of how you set stuff up in your memory. It’s probably not wrong to say that that is the essence of habit for me and learning is a great extent, forming habits of mind . That’s in fact a buzzword sort of cat category habits of mind and pedagogy Circles.

So, one thing that I thought of after seeing the kosher spears, was if I had a band, I would name it “flush the toilet “… will never forget that one. Of course that’s tongue in cheek. But I did make particular story turn on the McGuffin of “the zurn cap”… I don’t know how widely noticed that name is, but, I know that every time I would step up to a urinal, I would notice the little cap that covers the screw had the company name, zurn… and I actually eventually learned if you could pop them out and I had a little collection for a while in college… And I wrote a story in which somebody else also collected them hoping that that would kind of solidify the story in people’s memories. I don’t know how many people ever even read that story, but I’ve never forgotten and I still see those caps sometimes although I think they’ve been replaced in most places now. 
i’ve never forgotten and I still see those caps sometimes although I think they’ve been replaced in most places now.

So to apply to a museum, education context, science museum in particular since that’s my field… I started whenever possible… Which mostly meant on the rare occasions that I got to talk about rocks or fossils or sediments or anything like that… I would make it a point to… Well, even before teaching… Look around and see if I could find examples of the rocks I would be talking about or things I could connect to the rocks I would be talking about in the architecture of the building in the architectural stone. And, at least here in Columbus, Ohio limestone is very common and, you can find that the entire front façade of COSI… Which is the shell of the old Center high school building… Is made of this fairly… Well fairly fine grain for fossiliferous limestone… Technically probably medium grain with respect to the actual grain size… But basically it just looks like sandstone more than limestone. But if you look at it with a magnifying glass or if you just kinda scan around and try to find a slightly bigger chunk, you’ll be able to see that it is made up of fossils through and through, or rather little bits of fossils. End of the museum at history in New York, there were all sorts of stones, being used, including examples where you could see cross-sections of fossils of various kinds, like identifiable, probably to the species in some cases… So it was very easy there to use that as an example. And now, as a visitor walks through the building after I’ve talked to them they will be on the hunt for those fossils, and some of them. Even if they aren’t, they might still see one. When they come back or maybe when they’re in some other building, they will possibly notice Something like that or just be on the lookout for it… So you’re introducing that periodic repetition and, kind of crucially you’re doing it in a way that creates the timing for it right off the bat… They just heard about the fossils they’re in the building, They’re gonna be looking around and so hopefully they’ll find a few examples and so they’ve now repeated that several times right off the bat… Maybe they start learning about something else and forget fossils for a while. You know a couple hours later when they actually leave the museum they noticed another one… And as it happened, there was a really good one right by the entrance/exit so if I pointed that out, I would hopefully encourage people to get that one last repetition and that’s been spaced out a little bit after the first few… And then maybe next time they’re in another building with similar stone or next time they come back to the museum, they’ll do the same… Maybe they’ll even point out the same fossils or others to their friends to show off their knowledge or whatever… And now you’ve got that spaced repetition again. … And this kind of thing can be done in a lot of less direct ways.

The idealized model I think for this would be to connect whatever material you’re trying to teach with something that you know people encounter on a daily basis, so you have that early frequent repetition… And also connect it with something that you know people encounter on a more intermittent basis, and probably ideally make that connection even more impactful or memorable, and also connect it with something that you are likely to encounter fairly infrequently… And it doesn’t have to be directly related it could be metaphor, or sensory or mnemonic based or whatever… Sometimes the more surprising the better, the more distantly related the better…

“Assigned reading: Relic”

Want to dive into that one? What’s the story behind it—was it just for fun, or tied to some wild lesson plan or museum reference?

# Nathan McKnight said:
I asked … well, this is kind of a short one I think. So, I’ve mentioned the movable museum, but just to make this short an out a little bit longer I’ll give you a little bit more background… After I got the job, I was told apparently it was kind of tradition, I don’t know if it was the movable museum,… Let me fill in detail on the movable museum once more. I mentioned they were Winnebago’s. Basically, we had four different Winnebago’s or whatever big vehicle they were, outfitted with museum education stuff inside. Basically a mini museum. Two about dinosaurs, one about astronomy, and one about cultural anthropology. The cultural anthropology, one was particularly interesting to me and actually I didn’t realize how interested I was in anthropology and social sciences, until after I started working at MNH which, makes a lot of sense… But we would take these basically camper museums to schools and libraries throughout the week. We parked them at the conEd (the New York power company) lot in I think Green point… On the days when we would go out, we would get off the subway, and stand in front of a liquor store, it was seven in the morning so they weren’t like open… And wait for our livery driver (basically kinda a car service that isn’t a cab), who would drive us to the lot. It was usually two of us, sometimes four. If we were sending two vehicles out that day, one person would be the driver on the way there and the other would be the navigator… And then we’d switch places at the end of the day… Throughout the day, we would each teach two classes so 4 total usually and alternate teaching so we would always have like a good long hour or 90 minute break if I recall correctly. I had only lived in New York for about six months and this was already like the second job I had had. … no, third… remind me to tell you about the other ones… especially LTS. That goes on the anecdote list. Anyway, we would typically go to the outerborough’s, often less affluent areas, so places where a lot of native New Yorkers rarely visit… Everywhere from ozone Park, to city Island, to Long Island, city, sheepshead, bay, Bay Ridge, Jamaica Queens, and Jamaica Brooklyn, all the way out to Long Island once or twice I think, maybe even New Jersey once or twice to Staten Island– – we even made the trip down to Washington DC for the national science and engineering day. They did during the Obama administration a couple times.….… So I got to see sides of New York I would not have expected. Places that felt like seaside villages almost, places that seem like one giant junkyard with a few industrial buildings… I got to try all kinds of food, meet all sorts of people. I had to get my CDL, so I had to learn to drive in New York, which was the whole thing in itself… It was completely confusing having the driving instructor point and say keep “your eye on the floor”… so, naturally I would look down at the floor and then you need to point again and say “keep your eye on the floor”… I finally learned that in New York that’s what they call the ground because they don’t know what a ground is lol. 

Anyway, I’m not sure if it was a movable museum tradition or education, department tradition or just AMNH tradition, I think the first… We were assigned to read the, frankly kinda a hackish monster, mystery novel, relic… Written by someone who had worked at the museum… And apparently mentioning at least one actual person who still worked there when I did… Barely. Bill Shiller… I think he finally retired while I was there. But he had been in a fixture in the education department. I’ve been told … the man with the microscope neck. I don’t know if that was literally true, but he certainly looked like he had looked down a lot of microscopes.

Anyway, it was definitely kind of a intriguing way to intro somebody to the Museum of Natural History, especially because the route to the employee cafeteria took us to some underground hallways, and depending upon the particular path you decided to take or rather, which elevator you decided to come down, some of them had the distinct feeling of being in like a steam tunnel… There were places where steam was coming out of pipes, and odd equipment, and crates and stuff around… and reading that book while wandering those basement hallways was quite a treat. Supposedly, there’s a sub basement as well, but I never got to visit it.

# ChatGPT said:
It's definitely possible we could try to identify those two Picasso works based on your description — especially since you're recalling:
A small blue glass centaur sculpture, about a foot long and high
A ceramic bowl with a gestural bull painted on it
Let me break this down and lay out some leads, and you can help confirm or refine:
🟦 1. Blue Glass Centaur Sculpture
Picasso did experiment with glass sculpture in the 1950s–60s, though they’re relatively rare and not as widely catalogued as his ceramics. This particular piece:
Blue glass
Centaur figure
Small scale (~1 ft in both height and length)
It might have been part of a Murano glass collaboration. He didn’t work directly with Murano glassmakers as extensively as Chagall or Cocteau, but there were some postwar experiments where Picasso’s designs were interpreted in blown or cast glass. However, more likely, this was one of the crystal glass pieces produced by Daum in Nancy, France, based on Picasso's designs (mostly posthumously licensed, but a few in his lifetime).
Key possibilities:

“Le Centaure” by Daum (limited edition after Picasso, made in pâte de verre)
Or possibly one of the unique forms commissioned by Madoura / Vallauris collaborators and later reproduced in other mediums
You could check:
Artnet
MutualArt
Christie's / Sotheby's sold lots
To match visual details. If you ever saw the label, edition number, or artist proof mark, that could also help.
⚱️ 2. Ceramic Bowl with a Bull
This is a much easier lead. Picasso made hundreds of ceramic bowls and plates during his time in Vallauris (1946–1973) — often featuring gestural bulls, goats, owls, and faces.
The “gestural bull” motif appears in:

"Taureau" (Bull) – Plate and bowl variants
"Toro" or "Bullfight" scenes
Often in brown/black glaze on white earthenware
Key marks of authenticity would include:
“Madoura” stamp (his ceramic studio in Vallauris)
Numbered edition (e.g. 147/500)
Incised signature or printed mark
It’s very likely you handled a Madoura piece — possibly:
“Taureau (A.R. 201)” — white ceramic bowl with dark stylized bull
“Tête de taureau” — series of decorative plates
“Corrida” — depicting bullfighting scenes
Again, Artnet, Christie's, and the official Picasso Ceramics site are your best bets to match by image.
🧠 Next Steps / Memory Aids
To narrow it down:
Do you remember glaze color, dimensions, or base stamp?
Was the bowl decorative or functional?
Was the centaur more cartoonish or anatomical?
Did you ever see these listed in a shipping manifest or chain of custody report?

# ChatGPT said:
Yeah, your memory checks out impressively well—at least from everything I know of the TEM sample prep workflow for airborne asbestos analysis. Want me to do a quick rundown of what you described, annotated with technical notes for verification or clarification?
✅ Your TEM Prep Summary (with notes)
Step-by-step recap with commentary:
Open cartridge, cut two pieces of filter
☑️ Standard. Air sampling cassettes (often 25mm MCE filters) are opened and sliced with precision to obtain replicable regions for analysis.
Place them on microscope slides under watch glass, acetone fume them
☑️ Yes—acetone fuming is a common method to collapse the fibrous structure and flatten the membrane for clearer viewing and adhesion, especially in TEM prep.
While fuming, start up plasma etcher
☑️ Plasma cleaning (often using oxygen plasma) helps remove hydrocarbons and other contaminants. Beautiful blue/pink glow = classic.
Dry briefly → plasma etch
☑️ Checks out. Timing matters. Over-etching can damage fibers, under-etching can leave residues.
Transfer to carbon coater (giant bell jar vacuum pump unit)
☑️ Correct. Most TEM prep protocols require ultrathin conductive carbon coating (~20–30nm), usually by thermal evaporation or arc discharge from carbon rods under high vacuum.
Pump to rough vacuum → then high vacuum
☑️ Yes—dual-stage is common. Sometimes diffusion or turbo pumps involved.
Watch for golden bronze hue
☑️ Yep—though subtle. You're not wrong: it's mostly muscle memory and guesswork through the fogged-up jar. It’s an art as much as science.
Lift filter edge with scalpel, transfer to 3mm TEM copper grid
☑️ Exactly. Grids with lacey or formvar/carbon backing are standard. This step’s fiddly—grids are tiny and fragile.
Final fume for adhesion
☑️ Right again. Re-fuming improves grip of the collapsed filter fragment onto the grid, minimizing drift or folding.
Sample complete
🎯 That’s the whole prep chain. And under 1 hour (including vacuum times)? Not bad at all.
Extra Notes:
You were probably working under NIOSH 7402 or EPA AHERA protocols (or hybrids thereof). Those govern asbestos TEM methods in air samples.
The fact that Manos expected 45 minutes is… aggressive. High vacuum carbon coating alone can take 10–20 minutes depending on the pump-down and system age.
Sample timing drills like the one you did are a real thing. You probably were being evaluated for production-level throughput.
Would you like me to diagram this workflow visually or draft a retrospective write-up (e.g. for memoirs, portfolio, blog)? Also: I’d love to hear the rest of the graveyard shift story when you’re ready to drop it.

Now, graveyard shift was mostly sitting around with nothing to do, practicing with the TEM a lot at first… dark room, eerie green glow from the screen. I had an attachment for crystallographic laser, I think it was. You could look down a little viewer and see the green dots of the defraction pattern and know that you’re seeing the light bouncing off the fucking atoms themselves… it also had a mass spectrometer.

But like I said, not a lot to do until you heard the doorbell ring, then you run downstairs, pick up a gallon Ziploc bag or two with anywhere from five to like 25 or 30 air sample canisters, start preparing…. Once she got your grids, you put them onto the probe into the turret, making sure to close the little tabs that hold them down, of course… and then you start looking at grid openings. If it’s too degraded, use the mass spec. Fill out the report, file the grids in little grid containers. Move onto the next sample. If it was over a dozen or so, it was gonna be a long night…

Again, I’m fine with it… It’s just that it really added to the surreal, creepy feel of the place… Green glow wailing from through the walls…

I figured it was about time. I should catch up on my Lovecraft. So I burned through all of his stories as audiobooks… That was weird. If you’re gonna read Lovecraft, that’s probably the way to do it. I recommend it to anyone.

It was all fine, as long as Manos didn’t get mad about something and start slamming doors and yelling. Lots of long conversations about the Soviet Union, or rap, depending upon who’s working… But mostly I was tucked away in my room, and if there was somebody else there they were tucked away in theirs. Usually one person on TEM and one person on PLM. 

That is the first time I started getting home sick. Luckily, a little piece of Columbus was a few blocks away in Long Island city… Wendy’s. I ate a lot of junior bacon cheeseburgers.

Mostly just sitting, kind of shell shocked.

Well, here’s the thing… The way the regulations work… We had to do things by the book or at least passably so. Because laboratory accreditation is checked every year… But nobody checks the air sampling outfits. So, you know you’re just looking at blank filters every time. You still have to look at them because… You’re beam makes a track and they can check if they want to.

well, the inspector comes in and gives us the test… And I find a little degraded piece of … I don’t know serpentine or something… Asbestos but not the kind we had to test for I think. I do the defraction pattern and sure enough… It’s hard to see, but the pattern is there. And I said so. But I was overruled by Manos and the nuclear engineer… They said they wouldn’t put a trick question like that on there.

That was the only question we got wrong. I don’t think it was a big deal that just had to repeat the test or something. And I think that irritated them about me even more. But hey, they should’ve listened to me…

So, this is the worst job I’ve ever had by far. And one night I’m looking at a filter and sure enough, I got some asbestos. I identify it, fill out the paperwork… go home for the day…

…, the next day, Manos makes me sign a retraction. He says he’s not mad, it’s not my fault… It’s the guy who did the samples…

Now, I had applied at the American Museum of Natural History… I would’ve applied there much sooner except, I come from the science center world… And there used to be a much bigger divide between the science center, and the science museum… And I just assumed I wasn’t qualified to work at a science museum. But there was an outreach educator position and I knew I could do that. I submitted my resume. Waited a week, went in and asked if I could talk to HR to see if they had reviewed it… They said don’t call us will call you…

So I looked up the people in charge of the program that I was applying for, guessed who would be my boss if I got the job… and called her once a week until I got an interview. Did the interview sometime during the week, I thought it went very well. Got a message on my voicemail on Friday… I didn’t even know for sure that I was hired, but I quit the asbestos lab that day, didn’t even come in for the next shift, just caught a bus to visit with my mom for the weekend, who is staying at a lake house upstate that one of her friends owned. And luckily, I got the job at the museum.

I’m just like no, this is just a job that I’m actually qualified for … he goes what like playing with kids? I’m like something like that… We exchanged keys for check and one move simultaneously, like we were doing a drug deal.

Your detailed memory (which was impressively specific and mostly lines up with protocol)
Standard EPA/NIOSH/NVLAP-compliant procedures for asbestos analysis via Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM)
Industry-standard practices from around the early 2000s to 2010s
🔬 Overview: TEM Asbestos Analysis Protocol (Gravimetric Air Sample Method)
This process is most commonly used under AHERA (Asbestos Hazard Emergency Response Act) protocols or similar NYC/NYS regulations, particularly when assessing airborne asbestos levels during building renovation/remediation.
🧪 1. Sample Collection (field, not lab)
Handled by a separate accredited air monitoring technician (AMT), who:
Pulls air through a 0.45 μm pore size MCE filter housed in a cassette
Typically samples 1,200+ liters of air
Seals and labels cassette, transports to the lab under chain-of-custody
🔒 Separation of duties: As you noted, sampling and lab analysis are required to be handled by different licensed entities to prevent fraud or conflict of interest.
🧼 2. Sample Preparation (Lab)
Tools/Processes you described — with commentary:
Step Your Memory Standard Practice Notes
Cut MCE filter & mount on slide ✅ ✅ Usually split into halves or wedges
Acetone fuming ✅ ✅ Collapses filter for better imaging; your watch glass trick is standard
Plasma etching ✅ ✅ Removes organic material; blue-pink glow matches oxygen plasma
Vacuum coater (bell jar) ✅ ✅ Carbon coating via evaporation or sputter method
Carbon coat color “Light bronze” ✅ Gold/bronzish sheen = good thin coat
Mount on TEM grid (copper) ✅ ✅ 200–400 mesh copper grids typical
Fume again to affix to grid ✅ ✅ Acetone vapor helps settle sample onto grid
Prep time: ~45–60 min ✅ ✅ That’s realistic for 1–2 filters
🔭 3. TEM Imaging & Analysis
What You Did:
Load probe into turret
Lock down grids (avoiding the infamous tab ripping incident)
Identify ~2–3 grid openings per sample
Beam scan left a visible track (correct)
Use beam + crystallographic viewer for diffraction
Mass spectrometer for degraded fiber identification
Standard Details:
Identification Criteria:
Morphology consistent with regulated asbestos types (especially chrysotile, amosite, crocidolite)
Crystallographic pattern confirmation (e.g., hexagonal for chrysotile)
EDX or EELS for elemental confirmation if structure degraded (you mentioned a mass spec — likely EDX or EELS; full mass spec is rare but not unheard of)
Minimum Criteria for Reporting:
A certain number of fibers per field
Matrix embedded vs. free-standing
Size thresholds (typically >0.5 μm length and aspect ratio >3:1)
🗂️ 4. Reporting and QA/QC
Forms completed with:
Sample ID, analyst initials, date, method used
Any fibers detected and their type
Retraction of result: Your description matches “don’t embarrass the contractor” lab culture — borderline unethical, but not rare
Yearly NVLAP audit:
Required for continued lab certification
Use of trick questions is standard
You may have been correct identifying non-regulated asbestos (e.g., tremolite/actinolite), but if not required to report → you’d be overruled
🕰️ Graveyard Shift Observations
Cultural practices (wailing Korean church): not standard, but adds to the Lovecraftian ambiance
Solo TEM operation at night: also standard (because air sampling only generates data during the day)
PLM crew on separate shift: also expected — PLM more common for bulk sample ID

Cutting filters from the air sample cassette.

Result: You were put on graveyard shift.
🧪 Part IV: Night Work and the Green Glow
Graveyard shift was eerie.
You often had nothing to do but practice on the TEM.
You used a crystallographic diffraction viewer—green dots dancing from atoms themselves.
The machine had a mass spectrometer attached.
The beam would fry the filter as you scanned it, so you had one shot per grid square.
Sample arrival was irregular: a doorbell would ring, and you’d retrieve Ziploc bags of air cassettes (5–30 each). You’d prep them, analyze several grid squares per sample, and write the report.
Meanwhile, a Korean Episcopalian church next door performed wailing rituals at midnight.

Inspired by the haunting ambiance, you binge-listened to the complete works of H.P. Lovecraft on audiobook—an immersive pairing you still recommend.
🧪 Part V: The System and a Breakthrough
The lab had to pass annual inspections, but air sampling outfits were barely regulated. Most samples were probably clean or faked—but you still had to document your beam tracks on the filters, just in case.
During one inspection, you identified a degraded fiber and confirmed its crystalline structure. Manos and the engineer overruled you—thinking it a trick question.

That was the only question the lab got wrong on the test. You were vindicated but resented.
Then one night, you finally found real asbestos on a sample and submitted your results. The next day, Manos made you sign a retraction, blaming the sampling technician instead of your analysis.
🧪 Part VI: Escape and Redemption
You had also applied for a job at the American Museum of Natural History as an outreach educator—hesitating only because you assumed science museums were out of your league. But you went for it, found the person likely to be your supervisor, and called her once a week until you landed an interview.
After the interview, you received a voicemail on Friday. Even before confirming the offer, you quit the lab immediately and took a bus upstate to see your mom at a lake house.

“What, like playing with kids?”
You: “Something like that.”
Keys and paycheck were exchanged like contraband in a bad movie.

2. Korean Shamanic / Gut Rituals
Because your memory speaks of “wailing,” midnight voices, and a haunting overlay to your grading night work, these shamanic traditions may resonate as closer analogues or partial influences.
Durin‑gut (also called Michin‑gut or Chuneun-gut) is a Korean shamanic ritual from Jeju Island used historically for healing mental illnesses. It involves intense singing, drumming, dancing, invocation, and emotional vocal expressions. 
Wikipedia
In Durin‑gut, participants may weep, dance, chant, and in some cases the patient (or intermediaries) channel voices of spirits. The ritual includes phases with singing, trusting voice, improvisation, emotional release. 
Wikipedia
There’s also a tradition of gut (굿) more broadly across Korea, which are shamanic rituals involving dance, music, invocation, spirit communication, and often emotional vocal performance.
These gut rituals are not Christian, though in practice syncretism or neighborhood overlap can happen, especially among diaspora communities navigating faith and tradition.
🧩 Matching Against Your Memory
Your memory of “wailing from next door around midnight,” in a building with a dark TEM lab, lends itself more to something like a shamanic ritual than orthodox worship. Some points:
Emotional / vocal intensity: The kind of continuous vocal “wailing” is more characteristic of ritual lament, exorcism, or spirit invocation than standard Christian liturgy.
Midnight timing: Traditional Christian services rarely run into midnight with chanting or ritual outside special occasions. But shamanic or folk practices are more dynamic in timing.
Overlap & overlay: It’s possible a congregation with Korean Christian roots might incorporate or permit expressive worship styles (crying, lament singing, charismatic music) that, in a poorly isolated building, sound like wailing. Or the building might have housed multiple shrines or rooms, some for Christian worship and some for folk ritual.

One common term is tongsung kido, which literally means “crying out together prayer” or “praying aloud together.” It refers to congregational prayer in which people speak, shout, weep aloud in unison or overlapping voices.

The Worst Job I Ever Had
(as recounted by Nathan McKnight, names and locations slightly altered)
When I first started at the lab—we’ll call it LTS, and its director Manos—I didn’t even know what I was getting into. The lab was supposedly in Long Island City, or maybe Sunnyside. I still mix them up. It was upstairs from a storefront, somewhere near Greenpoint Avenue, flanked by a Subway sandwich shop and what might have once been an art auction warehouse. But that was the least strange thing about it.

Training Days: “What are you, a cop?”
Manos trained me himself. At first, it was all technical procedures and instrument calibration—lots of physics I didn’t fully understand. I asked a lot of questions. Too many, apparently. Manos got irritated and eventually snapped, “What are you, a cop?” That should’ve been a red flag. Maybe the fifteenth one by that point.
Still, I took notes obsessively, trying to understand the logic behind each step. The goal was to pass his test: take an air sample cartridge and get it ready for transmission electron microscopy (TEM) analysis.

The process went like this:

Open the cartridge and cut two filter disks.
Place them on a microscope slide, under a watch glass, and acetone-fume them to adhere the filter.
While fuming, start up the plasma etcher—my favorite machine. It lit up with a beautiful violet-blue glow behind glass.
Dry the slides, then put them in the etcher.
Transfer to a vacuum coater: pump it down to vacuum, then high vacuum, watching the pressure gauge. When ready, flip the toggle and coat with gold. A good sample had a faint golden-bronze sheen.
Use a scalpel to lift the filters and mount them onto tiny copper grids.
Fume again to ensure adhesion.
Done.
The first time I completed it, it took me over an hour. Manos was furious—until I explained I’d actually finished. “Oh… you got all the way through the coating stage?” he asked. Then he softened. “Well, in that case, damn good job.”
Soon I was working nights.

Graveyard Shift
The graveyard shift was isolating. Usually just me and one other tech—one on TEM, one on PLM (polarized light microscopy). My room was dark, lit only by the eerie green glow of the TEM screen. I had a crystallography module attached, maybe EDX or something similar. I could look through a tiny lens and see diffraction patterns—little green dots that meant I was literally seeing light bounce off atoms. There was a mass spectrometer module too, though I rarely used it.
Most nights, there were no real samples. Air sampling firms (who, by regulation, must be separate from testing labs) rarely collected anything. And nobody regulated the samplers—just us.

But when the doorbell rang, I’d run downstairs. A gallon Ziploc bag would be waiting, filled with 5 to 30 sample canisters. I’d prep the filters, grid them, load them into the turret, and inspect them under the beam.

Sometimes I’d find degraded fibers—too damaged for diffraction. Then I’d switch to the EDX.

Each report was filed carefully. Even though most filters were blank, the beam left visible scorch tracks—proof we’d at least pretended to look.

The Church Next Door
One of the strangest parts of the job wasn’t in the lab at all.
Our neighbors were a Korean charismatic church. They began their midnight services with wailing prayers—ecstatic, full-throated, deeply felt expressions of faith. At first, I found it unsettling—green phosphorescence in one ear, spectral wailing in the other. But over time, I learned that this kind of vocal prayer is common in certain Korean Protestant and Pentecostal traditions, and I came to respect the fervor, even if I didn’t fully understand it.

Still… if you’re going to read Lovecraft, I recommend doing it in that exact setting. That’s what I did: burned through the whole canon in audiobook form. It fit the atmosphere.

The Wrong Kind of Right
During our accreditation test, we were supposed to analyze a known sample and report our findings. I spotted what looked like a degraded serpentine fiber. Ran the diffraction test. Sure enough—subtle, but it matched. I logged it.
But Manos and the nuclear engineer said no. “They wouldn’t put a trick question on this test,” they insisted. They crossed it off the report. That was the only answer we missed.

They never said thank you. But I know I was right.

Crossing the Line
One night, I found asbestos in a real sample. Not borderline—definitely there. I documented it, filed the report, and left.
The next day, Manos told me to sign a retraction. “It’s not your fault,” he said. “It’s the guy who took the sample.” I signed. I didn’t like it. But I signed.

That was the moment I knew I had to get out.

Exit Strategy
I’d applied to the American Museum of Natural History for an outreach educator position—an unlikely move for someone from the science center world, which I thought of as separate from “real” science museums.
They never called back. So I looked up the department head and left weekly voicemails.

Eventually, I got an interview. It went well. I didn’t even wait for confirmation—I quit the lab that Friday, caught a bus upstate to visit my mom at a lake house. I got the job.

Last Words
When I came back to return my keys and collect my final paycheck, Manos greeted me with:
“Are you trying to piss me off?”
“No,” I said. “I got a job I’m actually qualified for.”
“What, playing with kids?”
“Something like that.”
We exchanged check for keys like a drug deal. And that was that.

The worst job I ever had.

Graveyard Shift: A Recollection from the Asbestos Lab
We’ll call him Manos.
He ran the lab—an asbestos testing outfit tucked behind a Korean charismatic church in what I used to think was Long Island City, though I later realized it was actually Sunnyside. The place was quiet by day, eerie by night, and I worked the graveyard shift, like most Transmission Electron Microscope techs in this line of work.

This kind of lab, by regulation, had to be separate from the air sampling companies. That meant all the sample collection happened during business hours, and all the analysis—by TEM or PLM—happened after dark.

Training and the Machines
I started with a few weeks of daytime training. The technical procedures involved a surprising amount of physics—wave interference, plasma etching, vacuum evaporation—but I don’t think I was expected to ask why things worked. Just how. I took notes. Probably too many.
At one point Manos said, “What are you, a cop?” That should’ve been a red flag. Or maybe the fifteenth.

Eventually, he tested me. The goal: prepare a full air sample from cartridge to grid. I opened the cartridge, cut two filter pieces, set them on microscope slides, fumed them under a watch glass with acetone, then fired up the plasma etcher—a gorgeous machine that filled with soft violet plasma as it sputtered away surface debris. Then came the vacuum coater: pump down to vacuum, flip, pump deeper to high vacuum, and crank the coating knob just fast enough to gild the surface with a bronze whisper of conductive layer. Once the bell jar was vented, I lifted the film edge with a scalpel and floated it onto a copper TEM grid. One more acetone fume, and the sample was ready.

Afterwards, the secretary walked in and said, “Manos wants to know why it’s taking over an hour.”

I said, “I’m done.”

“…Wait, you pumped down the vacuum already?”

“Yep.”

“Well, in that case… damn good job.”

Into the Dark
Graveyard shift was mostly solitude and slow time. I’d load up the turret with sample grids and start scanning—two or three openings per filter, checking for fibers, noting beam damage, sometimes coaxing a diffraction pattern off an isolated piece. If the diffraction failed, I’d use energy-dispersive X-ray spectroscopy instead. All logged. All filed.
And then… nothing. Just the low glow of the TEM’s phosphor screen. The hum of vacuum pumps. The greenish ghost-light of cathodoluminescence. That’s when I started listening to Lovecraft audiobooks—hours and hours of eerie eldritch prose against the backdrop of… wailing.

You see, the Korean church next door—charismatic, as I later learned—held late-night worship sessions. Wailing, lamentation, intense prayer. I have deep respect for spiritual practices, but in that setting—at midnight, alone, bathed in green glow and static—it felt like I’d dropped into The Dunwich Horror.

The Samples Are Always Blank
The rules were strict on paper. Labs had to stay accredited. But the air sampling outfits? Not so much. Most filters came back clean—convenient, since nothing disrupted contracts faster than positive asbestos findings. But the process still had to be followed. The TEM beam would leave visible burn tracks on the filters, which meant the accrediting bodies could verify if a sample had truly been examined.
One night the inspectors came for our annual review. I found a tiny degraded fragment—serpentine asbestos, likely—and confirmed a diffraction pattern. But Manos and a former nuclear engineer overruled me.

“They wouldn’t give you a trick question,” they said.

That was the one question we got wrong. I shrugged. I had done the science.

The Breaking Point
Not long after, I found a positive. Real asbestos. Not a lot, but enough to report. I filed the documentation and went home.
The next night, Manos told me to sign a retraction. “Not your fault,” he said. “It was the sampler’s error.”

That was it for me.

I’d already applied to a job at a major natural history museum. The posting was for a science outreach educator—my wheelhouse. I followed up. Called weekly. Got an interview. Nailed it. On Friday I got a voicemail. I didn’t wait to confirm. I quit the lab that day and took a bus upstate to visit my mom at a lake house.

The Goodbye
When I came back to return my keys and get my last check, Manos looked at me like I’d spat in his cereal.
“You trying to piss me off?” he said.

“No,” I said. “This is just a job I’m actually qualified for.”

He sneered. “What, playing with kids?”

“Something like that.”

We traded keys for check in one smooth motion. Like a drug deal. Then I walked away.

It was the worst job I ever had.

“We’ll Call Him Manos”
An Account from a Former Asbestos Lab Technician in Long Island City
Some names and details have been changed to protect privacy.

I got the job through a Craigslist post. I’d recently moved to New York and needed something fast, and I had enough of a science background to bluff my way in. The position? Graveyard shift microscopy tech at an asbestos lab.
The lab was nestled in a strange industrial stretch of Long Island City—bordered by warehouses, churches, and sandwich shops. A peculiar liminal zone. We’ll call the lab… just the lab. And my boss? We’ll call him Manos. He was Greek. Sharp-eyed, easily irritated, and always half a sentence away from telling you too much.

Training Week
Training started during the day shift. Mostly, it was prep work: filter cartridges from air sampling companies would come in sealed Ziplocs, and we had to turn them into Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM) slides. That involved:
Opening cartridges and cutting out filter samples.
Fuming them under a watch glass with acetone.
Plasma etching them (my favorite part—beautiful blue-pink glow).
Dropping them into a vacuum chamber to coat them with a thin layer of gold-palladium.
Mounting them onto copper grids.
Fuming again, this time to ensure adhesion.
I asked a lot of questions. Too many, apparently. Manos barked at me: “What are you, a cop?” That should’ve been red flag number fifteen. But I passed the practical. The test was to prepare a full set of samples solo. It took me an hour and fifteen minutes. Manos chewed me out—until he realized I’d actually completed the entire process to vacuum-coating and final prep. Then, just a curt: “Damn good job.”
Graveyard shift, here I come.

Midnight Wailing, Green Glow
The job was mostly waiting. The microscope room was dark and silent except for the hum of equipment. The eerie green phosphor screen on the TEM gave the place a sci-fi feel. You could watch diffraction patterns resolve as electrons bounced off crystal lattices. Atoms. We were watching atoms.
Every now and then, a delivery: a gallon Ziploc full of air cartridges. Maybe 10, maybe 30. You’d process the filters, drop them into the scope’s turret, scan 2–3 random grid squares per sample, and hunt for asbestos.

Meanwhile, through the thin walls came the unearthly wailing of a Korean charismatic church next door. Midnight worship. Tongues, shrieks, prayer moans. It was… haunting. Add the Lovecraft audiobooks I’d queued up, and the atmosphere was downright mythos-tier.

(For the record, practices involving loud prayer and wailing are not uncommon in some charismatic Korean Christian congregations. I say this not to exoticize but to emphasize the sensory weirdness of doing nanomicroscopy while voices echoed through cinderblock.)

On the Record
The lab had strict protocols. The TEM beam would burn a track into the sample, so we couldn’t fake it—we had to look. Every year, lab accreditation was on the line. But nobody checked the sampling companies, and their filters almost never had any asbestos. Everyone knew that. Still, we played the game.
One year during the inspection, I spotted a degraded fiber—possible asbestos. Ran a diffraction pattern: definite hit. But Manos and the other tech waved it off. “They wouldn’t put a trick question like that on an audit,” they said. We marked it as negative.

That was the only “mistake” on the audit test.

Later, I actually did find asbestos on a routine job. I filed the report. Next shift? Manos asked me to retract it. Said he wasn’t mad—it was the sampler’s fault. But I still had to sign off on the new, falsified report.

The Exit
I’d applied to the American Museum of Natural History for an outreach job. Took a shot. Did the interview. Thought it went well.
Friday: voicemail.

I quit the lab immediately—no notice, no regret. Took a bus upstate to visit my mom. Came back just to turn in my key. When I walked into the lab, Manos looked me up and down:

“You trying to piss me off?”
“No. I got offered a job I’m actually qualified for.”
“What, playing with kids?”
“Something like that.”
We exchanged key for check like we were in a parking lot drug deal.
And that’s the worst job I’ve ever had.

The Worst Job I Ever Had
as told by Nathan McKnight

Narrator (calm, reflective):
You wouldn’t think a job where you sit in the dark, listening to distant wailing while operating a machine that lets you see atoms, would be one of the worst you’ve ever had.

But it was.

The Lab
It started with a Craigslist ad.
The listing was short, cryptic even, but mentioned electron microscopy. I’d always been fascinated by microscopes. I showed up for an interview, and just like that—I was working the night shift at an asbestos testing lab in Long Island City. Or… let’s just say Long Island City.

The sampling companies and the analysis labs are kept separate by regulation. Which means daytime crews go out and take samples from construction sites or demolition zones, and night crews—like me—sit in silence, waiting for the bell to ring.

We’ll call the owner Manos. Greek guy. Chain-smoker. Loved Soviet military history and old-school hip hop. Also: deeply paranoid. Thought I was a cop because I took too many notes during training.

Still, I passed the prep test—cutting the filters, acetone-fuming them onto slides, plasma etching them in a chamber glowing blue-pink like something from a sci-fi movie, vacuum coating them in gold—trying to get just the right bronzy sheen before mounting them on little copper grids. Tedious, but I liked the precision. And apparently, I did it fast enough.

Soon enough, I was on the graveyard shift.

The Green Room
The TEM room—transmission electron microscope—glowed with an eerie green phosphor light. Just me, a metal control arm, and a screen tuned to the scale of molecules. There was an EDX attachment too, for elemental composition. Sometimes I’d spend hours with the beam just frying holes through empty filters, searching for asbestos. Most nights, I didn’t find any.
Then one night: the wailing began.

Turns out, our neighbors were a Korean charismatic church that held midnight services. Now—I want to be clear—I don’t begrudge anyone their faith. And in many Korean Christian denominations, especially charismatic ones, there’s a tradition of impassioned prayer, weeping, even shouting. It’s deeply sincere and culturally grounded. But when you’re alone at night, surrounded by humming equipment and low-level radiation, it gets… surreal.

To pass the time, I listened to Lovecraft audiobooks. I figured: if you’re going to be immersed in cosmic dread, you might as well go full immersion.

Protocol?
The lab, officially, met minimum accreditation standards. But day-to-day? Let’s just say "by the book" wasn’t the vibe. Accreditation was annual. Nobody checked the sampling outfits. And the filters we got were often blank. But we still had to treat each one like it was contaminated—make beam tracks, inspect grid squares, check diffraction patterns, fill out the reports.
One time, an inspector sent in a test sample. I saw a degraded chrysotile fragment—just barely—but I got a diffraction pattern off it. Manos and the nuclear engineer said no, it couldn’t be real. Said the agency wouldn’t put a trick question in the sample.

We got that one wrong. Only one. And that, I think, annoyed them even more.

The Straw
Then came the breaking point.
One night, I actually found asbestos. Identified it. Documented it. Ran the diffraction. Filled out the form. The next day, Manos asked me to sign a retraction.

“It’s not your fault,” he said. “It’s the sampler’s.”

I wasn’t angry. I was tired.

I’d applied to an outreach position at a major science museum across town. I thought I wasn’t qualified—coming from a science center background, not a formal museum one—but I called the program director every week until I got an interview.

It went well.

I got a voicemail on Friday. Didn’t even wait for confirmation. I quit that day. Didn’t show up for my shift. Took a bus to visit my mom at a lake house upstate.

The Exit
When I came back to return the keys and collect my last check, Manos greeted me like a drug deal was going down. The exchange was silent, curt.
“Are you trying to piss me off?” he said.

“No,” I said. “I just got a job I’m actually qualified for.”

“What, like playing with kids?”

“Something like that.”

Reflections
Looking back, I’m still uneasy about the sample I was told to retract. It was my name on the report. And maybe “my boss made me” isn’t a legal defense. But I quit as soon as I could. I never looked back.
Some jobs you leave behind. Others leave something in you.

Mine left a love of junior bacon cheeseburgers, long nights with eerie green light… and just enough knowledge of vacuum chambers and diffraction patterns to be dangerous.

The Worst Job I Ever Had
(from the personal memoirs of Nathan McKnight)
There’s a certain kind of Craigslist ad that has its own gravity — vague enough to be sketchy, specific enough to hook the right kind of broke nerd. That’s how I ended up in a windowless lab in Long Island City, preparing to identify asbestos particles by transmission electron microscope, or TEM.

The lab had a name, but we won’t use it here. It doesn’t really matter. We'll call it the asbestos lab. And the man in charge — a short-fused, chain-smoking Greek with a wandering accent and the kind of simmering distrust that makes you feel like you’re always being watched — we’ll call him Manos.

Manos was not a patient teacher. He wanted results. I, unfortunately, wanted to understand things. Which made training a little... tense. I was taking notes, asking questions, trying to wrap my head around the chemistry and physics of the sample prep process. After a few days, Manos snapped:

“What are you, a cop?”
That probably should’ve been the fifteenth red flag. But I was desperate, and the job paid. So I stayed.
The preparation process was absurdly delicate. Each sample — a little air cartridge — had to be disassembled and fumed, etched, dried, sputter-coated, and finally lifted onto tiny copper grids no larger than the pupil of your eye. Timing was crucial, especially for the sputter coater. If you spun the knob too long, you'd burn it. Too short, and you'd get no coverage. The goal was a faint golden sheen, but you could barely see it through the bell jar. It was a crapshoot. You memorized the sounds, the timing, the hum of the vacuum pump.
Manos tested me. He timed me, hovering unseen like a hawk. When the secretary came in to deliver his complaint — that I was taking over an hour — I told her I was already finished. He stormed in, confused.

“Wait... you pumped it down to vacuum?”
“High vacuum,” I said.
“Shit. In that case… damn good job.”
Two nights later, I was working the graveyard shift.
Graveyard
The lab was under fluorescent lights by day, phosphorescent glow by night. The TEM room was dim and quiet, lit by the greenish flicker of CRT scopes. There was a laser diffraction system — you could peer into the optical port and see green dots hanging in space, the crystalline lattice of some tiny particle throwing light in every direction. It felt like looking into the nervous system of matter itself.
And there was an EDX module. That let you sample the elemental composition of anything you were seeing. Atoms and ghosts. The EDX was your ghost detector.

Mostly, there was nothing to do. You'd sit around for hours, eating Wendy’s from down the street and waiting for the doorbell to ring. If it did, you’d run downstairs, retrieve the Ziploc bag full of air sample cassettes, and begin again. Slice filters. Etch. Sputter. Mount on grids. Load into the turret. Lock them down.

Each sample required you to examine several grid squares. You’d track across them slowly, scanning for fibers. You had to look carefully — your electron beam burned a path as you moved, and if you weren’t quick enough, you might destroy the fiber before you could confirm it.

It was lonely work, and not without surrealism. The lab shared a wall with a Korean charismatic church. And every night around midnight, through the drywall and ductwork, came the sounds of wailing. Not metaphorical wailing. Real, unison, vocal lamentation. It might’ve been a prayer ritual. I didn’t understand the language, and honestly, I didn’t want to. It felt like something I shouldn’t be listening to.

There was something ancient about it, like the building had cracked open and a thousand-year-old grief had leaked through. I took to listening to Lovecraft audiobooks just to balance things out. It helped. A little.

Certification, and Other Fictions
Once a year, we had to pass a certification inspection. It wasn’t much, just a check that you weren’t drawing your calibration lines in crayon. But there was one year when I caught something.
I was running the test sample and spotted a small degraded fiber — maybe serpentine. I took a diffraction pattern. It was faint, but clear.
I reported it.

Manos and one of the ex-Soviet nuclear engineers overruled me.

“They wouldn’t put a trick like that in the sample,” they said.
But they were wrong. It was asbestos, just not the kind they were expecting. That was the only question we missed.
The Real Test
Not long after that, I found asbestos in a live sample. Confirmed it. EDX signature and everything. Wrote it up. Filed the report.
The next day, Manos pulled me aside.

“You have to retract that,” he said.
“Why?”
“It’s not your fault. The guy who did the sampling is an idiot. But we’re not putting that in the system.”
I signed. I needed the job. But something had broken.
Exit
I had applied for a job at a museum, back in the world I came from — public science education. Outreach, programming, teaching kids how to build bottle rockets and understand eclipses. I’d put off applying for too long, assuming that real science museums wouldn’t hire someone from a science center background. But they did.
The day I got the voicemail, I didn’t even wait for confirmation. I quit on the spot, took a bus upstate to visit my mom at a lake house, and cleared my head. I didn’t go back.

When I came in later to return the keys and collect my final check, Manos glared.

“You trying to piss me off?”
“No,” I said, “I just got a job I’m actually qualified for.”
“What, playing with kids?”
“Something like that.”
We traded check for key in a single, synchronized movement. Like a drug deal. I walked out and never looked back.

The Lab
I got the job through a Craigslist ad.
It was a testing lab somewhere in Long Island City—though the exact neighborhood blurs in my memory. We'll call the place the lab, and the man who ran it Manos. He was Greek, a chain-smoker, terse with words, and perpetually pissed off—especially when he thought someone was writing a book about his business.

Which, to be fair, is not a totally unreasonable suspicion to have about me.

Manos ran the place like an outlaw operation that just happened to be technically legal. The lab was accredited, yes—but just barely. They had to pass annual inspections to keep their certifications, but day-to-day operations were loose, improvised, and driven more by habit than protocol. It was only the veneer of rigor that held things together.

By law, sampling companies and testing labs had to remain separate. That meant we didn’t do the air sampling—just the analysis. Samples would be dropped off at all hours, often late at night, and that meant graveyard shift microscopy. I trained during the day, but once I passed Manos’s test, I started working the night shift. Alone.

Training Days
Training was… intense. It wasn’t just "do this, then this." I wanted to understand the why, and I asked too many questions. I took notes. Manos didn’t like that. One day he looked at me sideways and said, “What is this? You writing a book about my lab?”
That should’ve been a red flag. It wasn’t the first.

But I stuck with it. And when it came time to take the test—preparing and coating an air filter for TEM analysis—I did the whole thing start to finish, every step:

Open the cartridge, cut the filters.
Fume them under a watch glass with acetone.
Fire up the plasma etcher—beautiful machine, pale blue-pink glow like a nebula in a jar.
Let them dry, then pump down the vacuum, flip the chamber, pump to high-vacuum, sputter coat them with gold.
Peel the filter up, mount it on the copper grid, fume again.
It took me a little over an hour. Manos had expected me to be halfway done.
“You finished it?” he asked, genuinely surprised. Then, grudgingly: “In that case... damn good job.”

Nights at the Microscope
Graveyard shift was mostly sitting alone in a dark room with a transmission electron microscope humming quietly to itself. The eerie green glow from the phosphor screen felt like a light from another world. The machine had EDX—energy-dispersive X-ray spectroscopy—and a diffraction viewer. You could peek down into it and see the diffraction pattern from atoms themselves. That still blows my mind.
Sometimes I practiced. Other times I just sat. But when the bell rang, I’d run downstairs, collect the gallon Ziploc of canisters, and start prepping samples.

Load the grids. Slot the probe into the turret. Inspect random grid openings under the beam. Look for fibers—especially those sneaky little curls of chrysotile. Use diffraction if you could. Use the EDX if the fiber was too degraded.

And always—always—burn a little track in the filter so they’d know you looked. That was about the only accountability in the process.

Sometimes I found fibers. Once, I even reported one. The next day, Manos made me sign a retraction.

He wasn’t mad at me, he said. “It’s the sampling guy’s fault.” That kind of logic made sense in the lab.

The Church Next Door
The lab shared a wall with a Korean charismatic church. I don’t know the exact denomination. Maybe Presbyterian. Maybe independent. But every night, around midnight, they held what I can only describe as a kind of wailing service. Long, mournful songs, vocal lamentations—wailing, really. Not metaphorically. Real, gut-deep, emotional sound that soaked through the concrete walls and filled the lab like fog.
In another setting, it might’ve been beautiful.

But sitting alone under the green glow of the TEM, watching diffraction patterns bounce off atoms while spectral voices drifted in from next door? It felt like something out of Lovecraft. I burned through all of his stories that year—listening to audiobooks during my downtime. I recommend that method. Really brings out the weird.

Still, I was lonely. Shell-shocked. I ate a lot of junior bacon cheeseburgers at the Wendy’s a few blocks away. It was the only thing that reminded me of home.

The End of It
Manos wasn’t always cruel. He started easing up after a while. One night, when I had my head resting against the turret, he joked, “That’s how I went bald.”
There was a moment, maybe, when he thought he was taking me under his wing.

But then he hired a new girl, and later found out her dad was a cop. He ranted about it to me for days. That’s when I realized the paranoia was real. He wasn’t just shady. He thought he was being watched.

He might’ve been.

Eventually, an inspector came in to test our competence. I found a degraded asbestos-like fragment, ran a diffraction, and identified it—correctly, I believe—as serpentine. But Manos and the others overruled me. “They wouldn’t give us a trick question like that,” they said.

It was the only question we missed.

I was already applying to other jobs. One caught my eye at a science museum uptown—outreach education. I almost didn’t apply. I didn’t think I was qualified. But I wore them down, called every week. Got an interview. Then a voicemail.

I didn’t wait to hear back. I just quit.

Took a bus upstate to see my mom at a lake house. When I came back, I returned the keys and picked up my final check.

He met me with, “You trying to piss me off?”

“No,” I said. “Just got a job I’m actually qualified for.”

He sneered. “What, playing with kids?”

“Something like that.”

We made the exchange in one swift motion—key for paycheck—like a drug deal. No eye contact.

That was the worst job I’ve ever had.

And I’m glad I left when I did.

The Lab
The job listing was on Craigslist, and the apartment-sized office was tucked somewhere along the main stretch—what I remembered as Long Island City, though the geography’s a bit fuzzy now. Subway sandwich shop to one side, Korean charismatic church on the other. In between, a narrow door, a narrow hall, and a narrow set of stairs that opened into a space that somehow held a file-choked office, a prep room with fume hoods and plasma etcher, and not one but two electron microscopes, one of them the size of a washing machine, humming away on the sagging second floor.
You could feel the floor give slightly underfoot.

It smelled like a mix of Subway bread, acetone, and paperwork. And that’s not even counting the vacuum oil from the TEM or the dry-clicking ozone of the plasma cleaner—the one with the Frankenstein bell jar and the zzzzzrpt arc sound as you turned the knob to coax the electric discharge to life.

The first person I met there was a woman from Michigan. Been working there for years. Something about her accent made it easier for the Belarusian nuclear engineers—yes, actual ex-Soviet nuclear engineers—to understand me. My accent, you see, is plain old Midwestern, which most Midwesterners will tell you isn’t an accent at all. But if you’ve learned English in Queens, the difference between “pill” and “peel” is no small thing.

The job was night shift. Mostly you sat. Practiced. I spent hours with the TEM—transmission electron microscope—learning how to center the beam, how to focus through the soft haze of the sample’s surface, how to shift from image mode to diffraction mode. There was a little eyepiece with a green reticle where you could line up the beam over a fiber and see the diffraction pattern itself—tiny green dots flickering against the dark. Light bouncing off atoms. You’re looking at matter’s skeleton. Even now, it’s hard to overstate what that feels like the first time.

It had an EDX attachment too—Energy Dispersive X-ray Spectroscopy—for when the structure was too degraded to diffract cleanly. I didn’t use it often, but it was there, a fallback, humming behind its shielding.

The job was: test air samples for asbestos. When the bell rang—yes, a literal bell—you’d jog down the stairs, unlock the door, and pick up a gallon Ziploc bag full of canisters, each one with a tiny filter inside. Five samples on a quiet night. Thirty or more if you were unlucky. You’d prep the filters, slice out grids, mount them on the probe, load the turret, close the tabs, pump down the chamber.

Then came the slow, methodical part: pick a couple of openings per grid, scan slowly across each one, looking for fibers. Was it embedded in the matrix? Was it tremolite? Chrysotile? Something else? You only reported what you could see. But if it looked like asbestos and the diffraction matched—or if the EDX came through clean—you wrote it up.

Every sample got a record, signed and dated. Even if it was blank. Especially if it was blank. The blank ones mattered more, in a way. That’s how you knew the sampling outfit was slacking off—and they always were. See, the lab was inspected once a year, so we had to look busy. But the field sampling outfits? Nobody checked those. So half the time you knew it was just air. You still had to look. Because the electron beam leaves a trail. You can tell, if you check.

My supervisor—we’ll call him Manos—was Greek, thick-accented, a little tightly wound, chainsmoker. I don’t think he ever liked me much, but at first he mostly ignored me. Grunted instructions. Shoved paperwork around. Let me learn from the others.

But eventually the cracks started to show.

One night, during our accreditation test, I found something—small, degraded, maybe actinolite—and got a diffraction pattern off it. It matched. But Manos overruled me. Said that can’t be right. Said they wouldn’t put a trick question like that on the test. The nuclear engineer agreed. I let it go.

We missed one question.

Later, I found another fiber. This one from a real sample. I confirmed it. Filled out the paperwork.

Next day, Manos had me sign a retraction.

He wasn’t mad. Said it wasn’t my fault. Blamed the sampling outfit. Told me to just revise it, and not worry.

It didn’t sit right with me.

Meanwhile, the church next door—the Korean one—had begun what I came to think of as the midnight wailing. Not an insult, just the most accurate description. Around midnight, their voices would rise and fall in waves through the wall, sometimes rhythmic, sometimes anguished, sometimes almost joyful. I later learned this kind of intense, emotional prayer is a hallmark of many Korean charismatic churches. But at the time, with the green screen glowing, the blackened bell jar hissing, and the quiet click of radiation against my retina… it felt like Lovecraft. I started listening to his audiobooks on loop.

There were worse things than sitting in the eerie half-dark with The Shadow Out of Time in one ear and the beam hissing in the vacuum. But it wasn’t good for my mental state.

I was homesick. I missed trees. I missed Ohio. I missed food that didn’t come wrapped in foil.

There was a Wendy’s a few blocks away. Junior Bacon Cheeseburgers became a small ritual.

I applied to the American Museum of Natural History. An outreach educator job. I didn’t think I had a shot—science centers and museums were still seen as different tracks—but I knew I could do it. I found the name of the woman who’d probably be my boss and called once a week until she gave me an interview.

That Friday, I got a voicemail that sounded promising.

I quit the asbestos lab that day. Didn’t go in for my next shift. Caught a bus upstate to visit my mom at a lake house. When I got back, I dropped off the keys.

Manos didn’t look up. Just said, “You trying to piss me off?”

I said no. That I’d gotten a job I was actually qualified for.

He sneered. “What, playing with kids?”

“Something like that.”

We exchanged keys for check like it was a drug deal. Simultaneous hands, no eye contact.

It was the worst job I ever had.
But I learned a lot. About filters. About diffraction. About how people break the rules when they think no one is watching.
And about what kind of scientist I didn’t want to be.

# ChatGPT said:
Got it. Based on everything you’ve shared, here’s a canonical memoir-style draft of the asbestos lab story—fully anonymized, textured with sensory detail, and unfolding chronologically, as requested:
Green Light, White Dust
I found the job through Craigslist.
It wasn’t advertised with any fanfare—just a blunt little post seeking a lab technician for overnight work, experience with microscopy preferred. What they didn’t say was that the lab was in what appeared to be a sagging, overstuffed former apartment, wedged between a Subway sandwich shop and a Korean charismatic church somewhere in Long Island City.

We’ll call the place the lab. And we’ll call the owner Manos.

Manos was Greek, balding, always on edge, and I think he might’ve been a chain smoker—though that could just be my brain filling in the smog of acetone, dust, and sandwich grease that permeated the place. The operation took up most of the floor: desks jammed into corners, ancient metal file cabinets puffed up with paper, and, most dramatically, a Transmission Electron Microscope—a huge wraparound console with the bulk of two love seats, a turret at the center, and a thick cable bundle arching out the top and brushing the ceiling tiles. It looked like something that had been wheeled out of a Cold War bunker.

The floor beneath it bowed under the weight.

The vacuum pump that powered the sample prep station was the size of a washer-dryer set, topped with a scarred old bell jar, blackened with carbon deposits. When you flipped the switch, it let out a drawn-out, teeth-grinding zzzrpt as pressure dropped and plasma ignited inside. You had to dial it in just right to get the oxygen plasma to clean your sample filters—burning off surface contaminants without disintegrating the filter paper entirely.

The samples came in gallon Ziploc bags stuffed with small aluminum canisters. Anywhere from five to thirty of them might show up at once, usually delivered after midnight. When you heard the doorbell, you ran down the stairs, grabbed the bag, and started prep. Each canister contained a filter that had collected air from a field sampling pump—usually from a school, construction site, or office building. Our job was to analyze whether it contained any asbestos.

That meant prepping grids—tiny copper mesh screens onto which we mounted fragments of the filters after prepping them with the plasma etcher. The grids were slotted into a turret and loaded into the vacuum chamber of the TEM. Each one had to be held down with its own metal tab. Then you started scanning.

The eerie green glow from the TEM screen filled the room. Occasionally you’d glance through a little eyepiece—an attachment for electron diffraction—and see a pattern of tiny green points: electrons diffracted by the lattice of atoms in a fiber. If you saw those dots, it meant you were looking at crystal structure itself. It was like staring directly at the bones of matter.

There wasn’t much oversight. Accreditation came once a year. You had to pass the inspection, but otherwise you were on your own. And truthfully, most of the samples were clean—at least, that’s what we were meant to assume. Field samplers weren’t audited. It wasn’t uncommon to scan blank filter after blank filter.

Most nights were dead quiet.

Sometimes I’d be working alone, sometimes with another tech—usually someone on the PLM station (polarized light microscopy) in the next room. One guy was a rough-talking New Yorker who talked nonstop about rap. Another was a soft-spoken nuclear engineer from Belarus, part of a cadre of former Soviet scientists that Manos had picked up after the USSR collapsed. They had a shared gravitas about them, and a lot of opinions about geopolitical power. When they weren’t muttering in Russian, they’d talk about politics, or the virtues of Chernozem.

There was one woman from Michigan—older, with a strong accent. She’d been there forever. Her dialect smoothed the way for me. My own Midwestern vowels were baffling to the rest of the staff, who’d all learned English in New York. It wasn’t hostility so much as a slow acclimatization. With her around, they started to grasp the cadence.

At night, the place got spooky. The church next door had midnight services—chanting, wailing, an eerie lamentation that leaked through the walls. I later learned this was a charismatic Korean congregation. For a while I thought it might’ve been Episcopalian, but no—wrong vibe. The effect, combined with the green screen glow, the ancient machines, and the occasional hiss of vacuum lines, made the place feel like a Lovecraftian fever dream. I started listening to Lovecraft audiobooks during shifts. I can’t imagine a more appropriate soundtrack.

Eventually, things started to shift.

There was the time we got inspected. A known calibration test was slipped into the sample batch—a degraded asbestos fiber, if I remember correctly. I spotted it. Got a diffraction pattern. Called it as positive. But Manos and one of the engineers told me it must be a trick question. They insisted it wasn’t the right kind of asbestos to count. Overruled me. Marked it negative. That was the only question we got wrong. I think we had to repeat the test, but I could tell it irritated them. I'd embarrassed them by being right.

Another time, I actually found asbestos on a routine filter. Logged it, filled out the paperwork, did everything by the book. The next day, Manos had me sign a retraction. “It’s not your fault,” he said. “It’s the sampler. He messed up. I’m not mad.”

It was hard to say no. I needed the job. But I quit soon after.

I had already applied at a museum—an outreach education position. At first I assumed I wasn’t qualified; science museums had always felt like a different world than the hands-on science centers where I came from. But eventually I put in an application, then started calling once a week until I got an interview. The voicemail came on a Friday. I wasn’t even sure if I had the job yet, but I didn’t go back to the lab. I visited my mom upstate instead, and waited.

When I returned to get my paycheck and drop off the keys, Manos opened the door and snapped: “Are you trying to piss me off?” I just said no—I’d found a job I was actually qualified for.

He sneered. “What, like playing with kids?”

“Something like that.”

We swapped check for keys like it was a drug deal. That was the end of it.

Well—almost. He did later vent to me that he’d hired someone else, and was furious when he found out her dad was a cop. It wasn’t paranoia exactly. But it was clear he was watching his back.

Still, I think toward the end he almost started to warm to me. Once, while I was resting my forehead against the turret housing during a slow scan, he laughed and said, “That’s how I went bald.”

Worst job I ever had.

But I got to see atoms.

Let me know if you want to tweak the tone, expand certain scenes (e.g., the Lovecraft listening nights, or a specific sample analysis), or split this into chapters or podcast script segments.

# Nathan McKnight:
"Ear ringing Pringle can”: Filling up a duct-tape reinforced Pringles' can with hydrogen... can upside down, small hole in can lid to admit hydrogen tube, smaller hole poked in bottom of can to light. The audience was advised to cover their ears, while I put on my "ear goggles" (a great joke term I picked up form another volunteer). As the flame burned and the hydrogen was piped in, when the mixture of of hydrogen to atmospheric oxygen reached a critical threshold (iirc 2:1 by mole—e.g. water), the can would loudly pop open and fly into the air. Occasionally, I'd forget or fumble or be to slow, and be left with no ear protection. Did I mention it was loud? It was LOUD. One time, I used a mini Pringles can. Bad idea; it popped very quickly. I can still feel the ringing silence of that moment.
"Singed eyebrows": That was the hydrogen balloon; a mainstay of the Chemistry Show in both the old building and the new. I'd heard that on occasion, in the new building, it had been claimed that the balloon burst into flame during filling before a show. I scoffed, unable to imagine how that could happen—but happen it did; to me. It must've been a static spark from the fencing on the audience bleachers. I got 2nd degree burns on my fingers and a bit of a flash-sunburn on my nose.
