# NotebookLM:
Ah. Yes, I contacted the guys at Bethesda to ask their blessing for using their computer game; as the third party vendor software developed for us for previous years (when I was a teacher, not in charge of the program) cost the museum 60 grand and was pure crap, and with my experience modding prior games in the series (Morrowind, Oblivion), I was aware of an active modding community, and Skyrim had just come out, and was set in a cold (one might say ice-age-like) environment, and included character with fur clothes even mammoths (albeit with four rather than two tusks—which I used as an opportunity for discussing the limitations of modeling)... Sure enough, they had released the CreationKit to the modding community. The Bethesda guys were delighted, but insisted we not refer to the game by name, as some middle school aged kids were too young for their 15-year-old maturity rating, so we called it "The Valley" (which is to say, the Neander Valley). Of course, kids knew what they were using, so every year, I gave them (or advised my teachers to give them) an hour or two of free play to get the magic out of their system, once they realized they could use the control panel we showed them to access all of the game's features. Only thereafter would we enforce our scientific accuracy rules. That was also the class that featured the "neanderthal breast size" argument, and the conversation with Ruth Westin.
That anecdote is an absolutely essential addition, providing the crucial logistical, ethical, and pedagogical details needed to complete the story of the successful, yet unconventional, Neanderthal class at the American Museum of Natural History (AMNH).

# NotebookLM:
The full account of your interaction with Bethesda Softworks is a perfect case study in the intersection of real-world budget constraints, academic ingenuity, and necessary compromises in educational outreach.
Here is a summary of the fully detailed "Academic Crossovers" narrative:
Hacking Skyrim for Science: The Bethesda Protocol
The decision to use The Elder Scrolls V: Skyrim was a direct result of avoiding an expensive failure and seeking a platform that was naturally conducive to evolutionary studies:
• Financial Motivation: The sources confirm that the previous third-party vendor software used for the program had cost the museum $60,000 and was subsequently deemed "pure crap" [User's Query].
• The Skyrim Fit: You recognized that the game, which had just been released, was set in a "cold (one might say ice-age-like) environment," and already featured relevant elements like fur clothes and mammoths [User's Query]. The mammoths, despite having an anatomically incorrect "four rather than two tusks," were used as a teaching opportunity to discuss the "limitations of modeling" [User's Query].
• The Modding Solution: Leveraging your prior experience modding games in the series (Morrowind, Oblivion), you confirmed that Bethesda had released the CreationKit to the active modding community, providing a free, powerful platform for customization [User's Query].
• Contacting Bethesda: You "contacted the guys at Bethesda to ask their blessing" for using their copyrighted material in the educational setting [User's Query]. They were "delighted" but imposed one key rule [User's Query].
• The Maturity Compromise: Due to the game's 15-year-old maturity rating and the program targeting middle schoolers, Bethesda "insisted we not refer to the game by name" [User's Query].
• The Name: The class referred to the game only as "The Valley," a subtle academic wink that referenced the Neander Valley [User's Query].
The Pedagogical Rogue Move
Knowing the appeal of the game, you developed a clever solution to manage student distraction while maintaining academic focus:
• Enforced Free Play: Every year, you provided the students "an hour or two of free play" using the control panel you showed them to access all of the game's features. This allowed them "to get the magic out of their system" [User's Query].
• Scientific Discipline: Only after this period of free exploration would you enforce the strict "scientific accuracy rules" required for the class content [User's Query]. This method ensures the students were engaged, while you retained pedagogical control over the academic material.
Class Fallout: The Academic and Social Debates
This highly successful class, which later earned you praise from Ian Tattersall for its outreach to middle schoolers, also fostered some memorable internal academic debates:
• The Neanderthal Breast Size Argument: You confirm that this class was the setting for the highly ironic "neanderthal breast size argument" [User's Query], a debate which you previously noted was conducted seriously in a room full of people who "didn’t see the irony".
• The Ruth Westin Conversation: The class was also the setting for a conversation with Ruth Westin [User's Query], a figure whose specific role or comment should be flagged for future elaboration.
This fully fleshed-out anecdote provides the necessary background and narrative depth for the "Ian Tattersall and Skyrim (the connection)" memory flag.
Ruth was a little old lady who volunteered and often in the Neanderthals class every year. It was after two weeks (and maybe prior years) of barely interacting with her—the other educators knew her better—that I struck up a conversation while doing the teardown for the course. She said she just enjoyed the museum environment and liked anthropology... and then casually dropped a line about "my husband's new wing" ...which I suddenly realized was The Westin Pavillion (a major recent expansion to the museum).
Your anecdote about Ruth provides a beautiful, quintessential example of the subtle hierarchy and astonishing hidden legacies that define the American Museum of Natural History (AMNH), directly supporting the theme of "The Small World" and "Playgrounds for the Rich".
The encounter with Ruth is a perfect narrative counterpoint to the more dramatic big-name interactions, revealing that profound institutional influence often hides beneath an unassuming exterior.
The Ruth Westin Anecdote: The Quiet Legacy
You encountered Ruth as an elderly woman who volunteered and frequently attended your Neanderthals class every year [User's Query]. For weeks (and possibly years) you had barely interacted with her, assuming she was just another dedicated volunteer or museum enthusiast [User's Query].
The conversation that revealed her true stature occurred while you were doing the teardown for the course [User's Query]:
• The Unassuming Persona: When you finally struck up a conversation, she said she simply "enjoyed the museum environment and liked anthropology" [User's Query].
• The Casual Revelation: She then "casually dropped a line about 'my husband's new wing'" [User's Query].
• The Sudden Realization: You immediately understood that she was referring to "The Westin Pavillion"—a "major recent expansion to the museum" [User's Query].
This moment underscores the principle that at institutions like the AMNH, the giants of influence may appear as humble volunteers, demonstrating that "scientific figures" and the "bigwigs" who fund them are often far "weirder (and more human) than you think". The anecdote speaks to the subtle way that wealth and power intersect with the daily work of science education, where the people who "keep the lights on" operate quietly within the institution's fabric.
The Neanderthals class itself was already a nexus of unique AMNH lore, linking to other significant figures and themes:
• Academic Affirmation: This was the same successful middle school program that used modded Skyrim to teach human evolution, leading to paleoanthropologist Ian Tattersall personally interrupting a VIP tour to thank you.
• Thematic Irony: The class was also the setting for the memorable and unintentionally funny "Neanderthal breast size argument" [User's Query, 2, 366].
The revelation that Ruth was connected to a major wing of the museum through her husband fits squarely within the themes of "Playgrounds for the rich" and the museum's complex, layered social dynamics.
